GRAPHIC ACCOUNT OF A VISIT TO OF MANCHESTER'S PALACES OF INTEREST. INTERESTING DETAILS. THE Great City of Manchester is undoubtedly

largest centre of commerce in the world. It is. next to don, the most important clty in the British empire commercial enterprise is famous throughout civilisation, has even penetrated to the daricest recesses of barbaric humanity. Manchester is the centre of a population within 12 miles of her Town Hall as great as London, the whole t! which is engaged in the cause of labour. Day by day t powerful and mighty engines of steam power are. under the dual control of the mechmic and operative, busily engaged in contributing to the successful trading of this marvellously active and indubtrious community. lii my visit to this won- derful city I chanced to discover amongst the many gigantic and colossal hives of industry a well-known famous furnish ing establishment, •• THH PANTHEON."

Suffice it to say that" The Pantheon" is indisputably the very largest Furnishing Establishment in the North of Eng- land exclusively engaged in Furnishing It is not a Drapery Establishment, but is a solid, compact, massive warehouse containing over a million square feet of Showrooms packed from the basement to the roof by the Finest Stock of Furni- ture open for inspection outside the city of London. The des- tinies of this great establishment, I find, are presided over by Mr.f W. H. Worsley, the sole and absolute proprietor, to whose untiring energy and unflagging enterprise "The Pantheon "owes its high position. To visit "The Pantheon" took the tram from the corner of Mossley-street Piccadilly, going Alexandra Park way. In a very few minutes, after passing St. Peter's Church and the mammoth Central Sta- tion, I was informed by the guard of m v arrival at " The Pantheon." My visit being in the evening, I was amazed at the brilliant display of electricity, the whole of the vast building having just been splendidly lighted by the Manches- ter Electric Company, under the able supervision of Mr. Crews, electrical consulting engineer, of Cross-street, I was enabled to see everythingas favourably as in daylight. "The Pantheon " has immense show windows fronting Gay thorn over 100 feet long, forming a gigantic display. Not inc the invitation- displayed on the windows, " Inspection Invited," without diffidence I immediately embraced the op- portunity by walking inside; announced my desire to see " The Pantheon," though not wishlng to purchase. I was met in the most cordial manner by one of the numerous salesmen, who very courteously offered to conduct me. I was at once struck with

OVERWHELMING- SURPRISE, the extraordinary immensity of the stock being sufficient guarantee of variety. On the ground floor I found a well arranged stock of Bedroom Furniture of the better class, suites innumerable at from £3O to £l5O. Magnificent speci- mens in richly-figured pollard oak, American walnut and burr, satinwood, exquisitely inlaid rosewood,Chippendale and Sheraton. These stood on prominently. Struck with the grandeur of one large ollard oak Bedroom Suite, I ventured to ask the price, expecting it to be quite £4O) to £5OO, and was surprised to find the price only £l2O ; another Suite, by Goodall, which must have cost nearly £2OO, pece only £65. The cheaper bedroom suites I was intormed were displayed in another showroom reserved for the purpose. A summary of the stock on view is briefly 200 bedroom suites complete, over 1,000 bedsteads, 150 drawing-room suites. 150 cabinets, 175 dining-room suites, and over 150 sideboards, besides the thou- sands of bargains in all the sundries which are essential to household furnishing. My curiosity was next aroused in a visit to the Carpet Showroom, which has been newly refitted. Here I revelled in exquisite artistic colourings DISSOLVING THE SOUL in a sense of fovelines3. Carpets in all their gorgeous colours, Axminster, Persian, Brussels, Wilton, and Tapestry. at incomparably low prices. Axminster 2s 9d per yard, Brussels is 11d, Tapestry from 90. Over 503 Second-hand Carpets form a special plum for those requiring a bargain ; large Second-hand Axminster and Bru sells Carpets from 153 65 to £3 10d, according to size and quality. Adjoining the Carpet Showroom is the Linoleum and Oilcloth Depart- ment, fitted with rollers for the proper display of the pat- terns. Linoleums from Pcl to 2s 6d, Oilcloths from 65 to lis 11d. Inlaid Linoleums at 3s 11d. I was nosy conducted I upstairs to the Dining-room Furniture Showroom. I was impressed by the enormous display of Massive room Furniture, the bulk of which is magnifi- cently carved in has r. lief. reminding one of the artistic immediately Solid Din l n - merit displayed in THE ATHENIAN TEMPLES.

Magnificent productions by splendid old makers, such as Gillows, Garnett, Lamb Henry Ogden and Sims, ac., at positively less prices than the common new London furniture now so largely retailed by drapers, &c. For instance, I noted a magnificent 71t 6in Sideboard, by Devesten. Bird, and Hull, which could not have cost less than £lOO, price £l6 guineas. What possible chance has new Londotr furniture in competition with bargains such as here displayed? Next a splendid solid oak Sideboard, with brilliant crystal minors, ft. £l2 10s; original cost £75. You can positively obtain Sideboards, second hand,condition equal to new, from 30s to I En, worth three or four times the amount; and, better still, you can depend upon the stability. A common new side- board is practically worthless ; eracks may develop suffi- ciently wide as to almost disAcse the contents to your visitors. I should strongly recommend the purchase of a good high-class sideboard, second band, in preference to new. doesn't matter what price you wish to give,you can obtain a splendid bargain at Worsley's in Sideboards from 30s up to £lOl, all equally cheap and durable. The same rule applies to Diningroom Suites, Overmantels, and Dining Tables. The bargains in this department are simply wenderful A fine, handsome Diningroom Suite, in morocco, vitt couch, two easy chairs, and six single chairs, second hand, but In good order cost £55 for £9 103 ; this is a sample of what may be obtained. Of course there is ample variety in price, suites from £2 10s to £65. A complete Suite in solid oak may be had for comparatively a trifling sum. My attention was called to a Solid Oak Dining-room Suite, consisting of couch, two easy chairs, and six single chairs, in morocco; a magni- ficent Sideboard, Overmantel, and Dining Table, the lot for £25, well worth £75, and cest £lO3. This seems almost in- credible, but a visit will amply demonstrate the verification. I now hear a brilliant display of rapid evolutionary chords on a pianoforte, and am then by attracted to the New Pianoforte Saloon recently erected adjoining the Dining-room Show- room, I find that the proprietor is not only a remarkable energetic business man, but that he is endowed with no mean ability as a pianist, the performance of a Chopin Polonaise amply demonstrating this upon a maznificent Erard Piano- forte. The Pianoforte Seloon contaies all the beat known instruments by the highest class makers at a price t rom 23 to 30 per cent, less than music sellers' list prices. For instance, Kirkman's span brand new Upright Grand Cottage Pianos, with all the latest improvements, are offered at 24 guineas, a price hitherto unknown. A very fine Tricho:d new Piano— forte, specially made for Worsley's, is retailed at 14 guineas, also something new as to price. There are a hundred instru- ments on view, but the gems of the bargains are the Second- hand Pianofortes. Here you can get pianos from 50s. to £5O„ simply incredible valise. Go and fee for yourself; it will cost you nothing.but may profit you much. American Organs equally cheap. The Music Saloon contains a number of CHARMING PICTURES

By well-known artists, at astonishing low prices. There are on this floor varieties of sundries, articles of virtu,ornamental and valuable property of all kinds, but space forbids all I could write of what I saw The next showroom is the Draw- ing-room. and it is almost impossible to do justice to tha very beautiful and exquisite display of really High-class New and Second-band Drawing-room Furniture. THE VALUE OF AN .A.T...us.—Jimmy "Say, I got out of a bad scrape to-day." Tommy : " Did yer? What was it ? " Jimmy; "Somebody told me mother a feller called Mugsey ' broke a man's winder down de street. If sho only knowed d.at wuz me 1"

.. HIS LIFE-WORIK.—" Oh, papa, what makes old Mr. Grabenheimer walk so stoop-shouldered? He looks like a horseshoe." "I have heard, my son, that many* years ago, when he was a very little boy, no bigger than you are now, he found a cent," .

EVIDENCE.—Bingo "Bobby, did you eat that little pie your mother made for you yesterday?". Bobby "No, sir. I gave it to my teacher." Bingo: "Did she eat ia ? " Bobby: I guess so. There wasn't any school to-day." PROVED.—Wife "Don't you think I am more economical than I was a year ago?" Husband: "I haven't noticed it." Wife (reproachfully) : "Why, dear, I get twice as many things with the same money." Still she hesitated. "You are poor," she argued, "while I have a million." Springing forward impetuously, he gathered her to his bosom. I will marry you," he cried "for all that !" SHAMEFUL OVERSIGHT. Uptown; "Burglars got into my house last night and ` lifted ' everything of any importance—with one exception." Down- town "What was that ? " Uptown: "The mortgage." WORLDLY WlSE.—First Mite: "I'll tell on you!" Second Mite: "If you do, I'll tell on you !" First Mite: "Nobody 'll believe you after yer reputashun's destroyed." SOME people spenci enough time in grieving over spilt milk to earn another cow. THE RULE OF THRHE.—Mrs. Fussy: "John, how did you happen to buy a new umbrella to-day ? " Fussy: " You told me always to leave one at home and one in the oiliee ; so, in order to keep off the rain, I had to buy a third." NOT EXACTING THERE. (pompously, at Western hotel) : "I am Senator Biggun " Clerk: "That's all right. We e,are nothing about a man's bad character at this house, as long as lie behaves himself here, and pays in advance. THE price of beef is rising so fast that after awhile the rich man will have cows on his lawn, instead of fawns. Ballet: "Oh, I belong to the realistic school Paint things just as I find 'em, you see." Farmer Barnes: "Don't believe in that, young man; don't believe in it. Neighbour Brooks was jest that • kind of a fellow—painted his house without puttyin' up c, crack. An' now look at it !" A WARNING.—RuraI Ragges : "No, mum, I -haven't come to beg. I've come ter warn yer agin men travellin' round callin' theirselves tramps. They ain't tramps at all, mum." Mrs. Hussiff : "How am I going to know them?" Rural Ragges : "Easily enough, mum. They look as if they was really hungry. They won't ask yer fer money. They'll want to do a whole lot of work fer a bite ter eat. They'll do the work willingly. An' after they've eaten the scraps you give 'ern they'll go away lookin' grateful. Yer can't mis- take 'em fer tramps, mum." ENDING A DISCUSSION.—Mr. Grumpps (hotly) : "You must be crazy." Mrs. Grunipps (icily) : "Just what everybody said when I married you."

SivrYTHE : "Site wasn't sure which she liked

better, Jones or me' but she gave me the benefit of the doubt." Bluffs: "And made you happy?" Smythe: " Yes ; they have been married more than a year now." Mrs. Casey: "Let's immigrate to Americay, Moike. Do yer moind phwhat luck Pat Shanahan had? He was only there two year, whin he doied worth t'ree hundred pounds." , MR. Now we will have to 4 associate with a lot of nobodies who inherited their wealth." _ _

MISS KENNETH: "How is it they do not use the telephone in Russia?" Mr. Potter: "Well, you see, Hello' in Russia is Tzizakenfitkiajanjanski— hence the telephone has not been introduced into that country.' ONE GOOD POINT. A custom prevails at funerals among the Dutch settlers in Natal for some one of the deceased's friends to make an ,oration at his grave, recording his virtue's and the good deeds performed by him. It happened once, however, that an individual died who was not considered to have been possessed of any virtues whatever. Consequently the , obsequies were nearly being completed in silence, when one of his country said, in solemn tones—" Him was a berry good smoker." "Who is going to be the best man at your wedding ? " asked Hicks of Garraway, who is shortly ,to marry a "new woman." "I don't know, said Garraway, sadly. "Miss Strongmind and I have not settled yet which of us is to have that honour."

- REUBEN (telling of his visit to London) : " Wun day I sat right 'longside two burglars in a street kyar." Cynthia: • " flow did ye know they wuz burglars, Reub?" Reuben,: "Waal, wun sez t' the other 'l'm goin' to Rob 'Roy t' night; will ye cum?' and the other sez, Yeou bat I will. I hope it'll betas good a Robin Hood.'" A Possinmrrt.-=-How- true it is that a guilty conscience needs no accuser was well shown by the following little occurence. A gentleman went out of town for a day's fishing, taking a luncheon with him. When he reached the creek he discovered that he had dropped the luncheon somewhere on the Way, and hastened back to look for it. By-and-by he met a burly nego, who' looked very well pleased, and was brushing some crumbs from the breast of his coat. "Did you pick up anything in the road as you come along ?" asked the gentleman. "No, sah," answered the coloured man. "I didn't pick up anything. Couldn't a dog have found it and eat it up ?" A BRILLIANT IDEA.—An interesting incident occurred while a company of a certain battalion stationed in one of our garrison towns were going through their musketry training. ' Owing to a strong wind blowing from the right, the bullets kept falling to the left of the target. An .old major, who was in charge at the ranges, came over to the colour-sergeant and inquired the cause of the .bad shooting. On being told that there was too much wind blowing from the right, causing the shots to fall wide, he astonished the sergeant by asking—" Wouldn't it be a good idea if the targets were moved more to the left?" "CAPITAL" CEIA'L

The manner in which the police have tracked down the persons suspected of the Muswell Hill murder is undoubtedly a great feather in their cap. It does our detective force even more credit than they quite properly earned by their conduct in the robbery of silver ingots, and should go far to- wards silencing those who are ever ready to sneer at the capacity of our police to follow up slight clues and to piece together disjointed links in the chain of evidence. There was great excitement at Highgate on Monday evening when the officers ar- rived with their captives.

The calculation of every ene with whom I have conversed on the subject of the duration of the Licensing Commission is that it will last during the lifetime of the present Parliament, or till so' near the period of its natural dissolution that the ' present Government will be able to turn the pro- blem over to their successors. In the meantime, public opinion may have ripened in favour of some one or other of the many proposed methods of deal- ing with it, in which case, if the Unionists return to power, their problem will be much simplified. Meanwhile,the question is shelved for six years,and probably longer. There is a strong impression among a large number of temperance men here , that public opinion will set keenly in favour of the I adoption of the Gothenburg system. They are led to this belief in consequence of the growing pres- sure of local rates, and from the hope that if the local governing body can intercept the profits aris- ing from the liquor traffic it will do something to lighten the burden of local taxation. Certain it is that in seven years the amount of the local rating will be considerably higher than it is at present, and so long as the Tory party is in power the taxation of ground values is a vain hope. The Gothenburg system, moreover, implies one form of local control, though not exactly that conveyed by local option.

The travelling Emperor has reached Vienna, and there cannot, says the "Daily Chronicle," be much doubt that his dual mission to his two allies is meant to re-fasten the links of the Triple Alliance. So far as Italy is concerned that has probably been done. The present Government is less cordial to "La Triplice " than its predecessor; but to come out of tbe great European combination is more than a moderate Italian Ministry—with a gentle chief like the Marquis di Rudini—is likely to attempt. Italy therefore re-enters the fold with sadly diminished power and prestige. Have we, too, en- tered it ?.is a sufficiently pertinent question. Not formally, we imagine. But in fact and in spirit we are nearer to it, both as a matter of general policy and of emergency action, than we have ever been before. And that is a fact which the country ought to realise.

At first sight the telegraphed announcement that a "group of large capitalists residing in Fgypt " have "instructed counsel to oppose the action brought by the French Bondholders' Syndicate with reference to the advance of funds by the Caiss of the Egyptian Public Debt for the Nile expedition" seems rather flattering for British foresight and prestige, especially as the only two capitalists named are not of British nationality. But a little examination reveals an interesting chain of cause and effect. Messrs. Suares and Zeronduchi, the two capitalists in question, control one of the three large financial syndicates, in the hands mainly of Austrian and Italian Jews, which have been buy- ing up some of the best land in Egypt under the British occupation, and which have at the same time been cutting out resident British firms in :en- ders for Government contracts. At the head of one of the other syndicates is Baron Menasce, of Alex- andria. Apart from the general motive for desiring the British authorities to amicably widen the ter- ritorial area of their speculations, these " groups " or syndicates have a quite immediate and a prac- tical concern in the extent and availability of the "funds for the Nile expedition." Me,srs. Snares were applied to for railway material as•soon as the advance was ordered: They at once supplied great quantities of sleepers, and they appear to have been first in the field also with rails. If the responsi- bilities undertaken by this country in EYypt were to be dependent On the aims of these competing "large capitalists," the forward movement might go on for ever.

If we are to credit a curious piece of news that comes to hand in a rather round-about manner,this country has recently been on the brink of a Minis- terial crisis without being aware of the fact. As Mr. Courtney pointed out at Bodmin a few days ago some of the members • 'of Her Majesty's Govern- ment are believed to be doubtful as to thi3 wisdom of the mysterious Soudan expedition. But it was not uncertainty as to their new Egyptian policy that thtleatened Lord Salisbury's Ministry, but dis- union respecting the currency question. On the 16th ult., the day before the debate on Mr. George Whiteley's resolution, the London correTondent of the " Pioneer " telegraphed :—" Difference of opinion in the Cabinet nearly caused Mr. Balfolir and Mr. Chaplin to resign, Sir M. Hicks-Beach ob- jecting to their advocacy of the bimetallic pro- gramme as calculated to make the Ministry unpopu- lar." The " Pioneer" is well-known as the organ of the official classes in India, and its proprietor, Mr. G. W. Allen, is one of the vice presidents of the Bimetallic League.

The borings for coal at Dover have been con- ducted with perfect privacy, even the coastguard on the top of Shakespeare's Cliff. and within a stone's throw or two of some of the borings, having no information on the matter beyond the vaguest hearsay,and ,risitors being rigorously excluded from all means of knowiedge.' The veil is now uplifted by the syndicate conducting the operations,and the results confirm the surmise of geologists that the carboniferous strata of the western counties dip in a continuous basin which reappears on the otter side of the Channel in the great coal beds of Belgium and the north-west of France. The lowest of eight seams was found at. a depth of 2200 ft., and more seams would probably have been found if the bor- ings had been carried further down. This discovery will alter the whole character of the southern counties. The establishment of great mining com- munities in these southern counties,the strongholds of Toryism at present, will introduce also a politi- cal revolution into the villages, and break up the old Conservative fortresses, which have resisted as yet all the teachings of rural reformers. Such a re- sult will, in the opinion of some at all events, be well worth the loss of our green fields and the es- tablishment of two or three black countries south of the Thames.

A well-authenticated report reaches me from Paris to the effect that the French Premier has drawn up a Note to be presented to the European Powers bearing upon the British occupation of Egypt and the neutrality of the, Suez Canal. The Note in draft was, I hear, submitted to the Russian Ambassador in Paris on Saturday, and he. after MDking various alterations, suggested Anallv that before being issued the Note should be submitted to his Government.. This is now being done, and it is expected that the Note will be issued about the end of this week. It is said to be short, but of a conciliatory pharactpr so far as England is concerned:

Excitement runs high amongst the Noncomfnrm- ist leaders in London on the ..Government . Educa- tion Bill, and the antagonism to the measure deepens. I hear that Mr. R. W. Perks, M.P., has offered to open his private resiience at Kensington to a gathering of four or five hundred Noncon- formists, representative of the whole country; also that shnrtly there is to be a similar representative meeting at the City Temple. As regards the Wes- leyans, interest centres in next week's gathering at the Centenary Hall. The Rev. Dr..Rigg is likely to make a sutireme effqrt to put: himself _right with regard to the Purcell letter business, and rumour says there will be some plain speakirg on the part of Mr. Priceflughes and the more Liberal section of the Special Committee.

All rights reserved. TB.INGS A WOMAN W ANTS TO KNOW.

IN spite of the very general use of coats and skirts, there comes a time when a really neat fitting tailor-made dress is smarter than any- thing else. Bearing this in mind, and know- ing that at the present time you will be in all probability requiring something of the kind, I am this week giving you for ray first sketch a very pretty costume. It is carried out in grey tweed, and the coat bodice you will notice is double-brtasted; with a seam at the waist where a full basque is joined on. The revers with which the bodice is turned back are of medium size only, and are covered with' white cloth, the collar being of the grey. Notice here the reduction in the size of sleeves, which, although still somewhat full, are considerably smaller than of yore. The fulness ,is more at the immediate top, the sleeves being quite tight-fitting to the elbow, and even fairly so just above. The turn-back cuffs are of white cloth, matching the revers. White facings are extremely smart this year; those of white cloth porhaps look best, but on the other hand those

A Tailor-made Costume. No. 2053.—Bodice. Paper Pattern, sid. No. 2054.—Skirt. Paper Pattern, Bid. Complete Costume. Is. Op. To be had from PATTERN DEPARTMENT, 18, HENRIETTA. STREET, LONDON. W.C.

of pique—and there are many of these to be seen—have the additional advantage of being washable. Moreover, they are so made that they button on again inside the coat when required. Hats are immensely trimmed just now. The most popular shape is a fiat one with a some- what square crown. That in my sketch has a full ruching round it of Chine ribbon with a bow and osprey on the left side, and bunches of roses nestling in the fulness of the ribbon. The brim is turned up at the back with a wide bow of black satin, bunches of roses again appearing on either side. No hat is complete at the present moment without what French people calla ca c-peign e,which is the only name I can give to this trimming at the back of hats. As soon as spring appears we are apt to throw off our winter clothing, quite forgetting the truth of the old adage': "e'er cast a clout till May be out." Ido not believe entirely in this, for,l think that with the present fashion of wearing woollen underclothing, we may take more liberties than formerly, especially with our dresses, coats, etc. - -

There is another point on which people are very apt to be careless, and that is in children's night apparel. Many mothers deem it neces- sary to clothe their little ones in flannel dming the winter, but directly the snow disappears flannels are stowed away for another year. This is quite a mistake, for children are far more restless at night in summer than in winter. They throw themselves about, and kick off the bedclothes, often leaving their legs

A Sleeping Suit. (Age 6to 10 years.) No. 205. Paper Pat' ern, tid. To be had from PATTERN_ DEPARTMENT, 13, HA. irmireTr STREET, LONDON, W.C.

quite bare. Thus are chills caught, and we all know that a cold is more easily caught than lost. lam sure that children should wear flannel at night in winter and summer. Of course, if preferred, a lighter kind can be chosen for, summer, but I would not change the material. • Let me draw your, attention to the a'ccom- panying sketch of a child's sleeping suit: It is, I consider, one of especially go'o'd design, being made in ,the form-of a combination with feet coverings, so that no matter how much the child may kick about, it cannot become un- covered. The design is simple in construction, and by the aid of one of our patterns can be easily carried out. , - Nearly everyone appreciates pot flowers in sitting-rooms; so no doubt many people will be glad' to hear' of a simple flower - stand which can be easily made at home. A charm- ing one I saw in the house of a friend the other day was standing in front of a French window, and the effect was excellent. _

_ , It was made of a child's wheelbarrow of rather a large size. When bought it was of plain wood, but my friend told me she had transformed it into its present state. The process is as follows: First rub it all over with fine, sandpaper, thus making the surface per- fectly smooth. Then size it, and when thoroughly dry proceed to paint ,it with enamel. Each coat of paint must be put on as thinly as possible, and if the enamel becomes at all thick, reduce it with turpentine. Five or six coats of this rendered the barrow absolutely perfect. . This done, my friend stood her pots in it, placing some on 'other pots so as to make the whole a good shape; the interstices were filled with moss, and the result was absolutely charming. She further told me that when she wanted to water her flowers, all she had to do was to wheel the barrow into the garden, give them a thorough soaking, and when they were drained return it to the sitting-room again. The last time I saw my friend she was pre- paring another of these charming stands, but as this was intended for a present, she had a zinc tray fitted into the bottom, the sides being about three inches deep. It certainly was an improvement, but by no means necessary, and, if expense were an object, could quite bell be omitted. _ .

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY APRIL 18. 1896 THE HIDDEN HAND ;

'• WOMEN'S CHAT."

it is whispered that the Princess of Wales will take' part in the Season's gaieties, after her visit to Copen- hagen next month, and if so its success is assured. The cloud caused by the death of Prince Henry of Batten- berg has now passed away, and the Prince and Princess of Wales,and the Duke and Duchess of York having the welfare of the London Season at heart, intend by their own hospitality and patronage of entertainments in general, to set an example that will be eagerly followed by Society. The Princess of Wales is certaln to spend some weeks in the metropolis, superintending the ar- rangements for her daughter's marriage, and assisting her in the selection of a trousseau, worthy of a grand- daughter of the Queen, a labour of love, to which our charming Princess brings the exquisite taste which has ever characterised her own toilettes. Should a garden party be held in connection with Princess Maud's mar- riage, it will take place either in the grounds of St. James's Palace, or Buckingham Palace. Nothing definite at present, however, has been settled, but it is known that there is a keen desire on the part of those most con- cerned, to introduce this particular function into the wedding festivities. The. Duchess of Saxe Coburg-Gotha has provided Prin- cess Alexandra with a truly magnificent trousseau, al- though not quite so extensive as those made for the bride's two elder sisters. This is because the newly married pair will reside for some months quietly in the country,the gay season in Germany only beginning with the approach of winter, when the Dutchess intends giv- ing her daughter a further supply of wedding finery in the shape of costly furs. Princess Alexandra has no crown to look forward to in her marriel life, neverthe- less she is likely to have a brilliant as well as a happy time before her,for the Prince of Hohenlohe Langenberg is first cousin to the German Empress, and connected with many of the leading princely houses in Germany. Two of the most popular of our younger women ' novelists, neither of whom have been much en evidence lately, are busy upon new works. "George Egerton" is soon to publish a novel, the title of which will proba- bly be "The Burden of the Ill" and" John Oliver Hobbes "(Mrs. Graigie) will shortly be represented by a tale entitled " The Herb Moon." At present she is en- gaged upon a lengthy novel to be published in the autumn under the name of "A School for Saints." The Rev. Baring-Gould whose new novel "The Brown Squire "is not meeting with an over friendly reception from the critics, had a most romantic wooing. When a young raan, Mr. Baring-Gould worked in the Midlands, and fell deeply in love with a factory girl. He had her educated,and then married her, and all who know them say he could not possibly have selected a better wife. Beside being a novelist the Rev. S. Baring-Gould is a popular hymn writer, an ecclesiastical historian, and an authority on folk-lore and antiquarian matters. He is both squire and parson of Lew Trenctard in Cornwall— an estate of 3,000 acres, and Lves in the old manor house there.

By the way, speaking of Mr. Baring Gould reminds me of a London clergyman who quite recently appears to have hit upon a somewhat unique method for pleasing the majority of his congregation with regard to the selec- tion of the hymns. -Constant church-goers, we all knew, are frequently in the habit of requesting that his or her favourate hymns may be placed among the list of those to be sung, but in this as in most other cases, tastes differ, and what one worshipper likes, another does not. This particular clergyman, however, has decided that lists of twelve favourite hymns shall be asked for, and duly sent in and the dozen which stand at the top of the poll are to be used. It is to be presumed that after a while another electien will be necessary. There is now in smart circles great talk of a League, to check the tendency of modern women to dress beyond their means, often indeed, vulgarly and ostenta- tiously beyond their position. The means to effect this end are limitations as to the number of gowns to be worn each seas m. Personally, I feel doubtful as to the success of such a League, although quite alive to the necessity for a good thing of the kind. Dress has now become so costly that it taxes many a fairly well-off woman to the utmost to know how to keep up appearances in this London world of ours, where, appar ntly the one aim and object of our life is to appear ten times richer than we really are. At the present time there is only one way we can manage, comfortably manage, to look nice on small means, and that is, to set aside the absurd creed of not wearing the same gown too often and possess ourselves of a few really well-turned out things in preference to a quantity of cheap and manifestly third-rate garments. The Princess of Wales is often seen time after time dur- ing a season in the same gown, so are her daughters, so irlso is the Duchess of York, and among smart titled women I could give many instances of those who wear the same dresses time after time and yet always look well dressed,neat, and thoroughly nice. When speaking with a famous French dressmaker a few days back,l asked her to explain to me her method of starting new fashions. It seems, when she is anxious to introduce a new mode, she designs an exquisite crea- tion calculated to suit a leading actress, whe will carry ,it off with grace and elegance. The-design is carried out on a lay figure and submitted to the actress, who nine times out of ten accepts the subtle homage. The dress is seen to the best advantage upon the stage, is copied into the fashion papers and—becomes the mode. Unless some unforseen change takes place between

this and midsummer we may expect the medium-sized hat to be maintained throughout the season. What it lacks in size however, it will make up for in volume and variety of trimming. Only quite simple morning hats will be worn plain, flowers and foliage, ostrich and quill feathers, draperies and poufs of net and gauze, ribbons and lace, will be crowded on to all the other shapes. which in themselves are to be fanciful combinations et straw material. Plaid and checked ribbons are being shown in all the most up to date shops but although dressmakers intend to make use of them. I hear upon the very best authority that milliners will show them little favor, preferring shaded and flower printed ribbons with wide bands of black or some dark color on the edges,and plain ribbons of satie, taffetas, or cuir-de-sofe, or else watered ribbons which have re-appeared lately. All sorts of thin black fabrics with grenadine or gattz grounds are already being made up with a view to bright warm spring days, and although many are being built upon black foundations, colered liningsare more fashim- able. Effects obtained by transparency are the most up-to-date; positive tones being generally preferred for the opaque fabric underneath, and this arrant etnent is not only carried out in dresses butis extended to emart capes. It appears, according to a correspondent in one of the London evening papers, that the prevailing fancy for tiny watches is merely a revival of an ancient fashion. This learned individual goes on to remark that Queen Mary possessed an octagonal silver watch whose dial plate measured only nine-tenths of an inch in diameter, and that her unfortunate namesake Mary Queen of Scots took pride in a small silver death's-head, which concealed a dial in its throat. The works took the place of brains,and were wound up with catgut,and the lower jaw opened on hinges to reveal the dial. Just now when the house-cleaning season is once more with vs. I would remind my readers that yellow is the colour for rooms that have no sunshine. It should not, therefore, be used for wall coverings in rooms that are flooded with sunshine, especially if they are for living rooms. If a light covering is liked for the wall of a sts -,ny room a modified yellow in the shade known as cream or buff, should be chosen. A light olive is also a delightful medium shade, and combines perfectly with white, old pink. reds, or certain shades of blue and yel- low. Very well lighted rooms can also bear Indian or Pompeiian reds. They have a cool effect even in mid- summer.

Here is an excellent method whereby to press seams ; Take a rolling pin, cut it in half so as to make a fiat surface ; then cover it as one would an ironing board. It supplies just what is needed, a curved, smooth sur- face, yet one which remains firm beneath the weight of the iron. APPLE TRIPLE is delicious. Cut six penny sponge cakes in half and spread with marmalade that has bef.n made quite hot. Take the pulp of two pounds of apples:which has been stewed with sugar and lemon, and pour while hot over he cakes. When cold cover with custard and stick with split almonds.

WARRINGTON CRICKET, BOWLING, AND ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL CLUB.

Slnce Autumn's shadows o'er us fell Young men have kicked the football well, With sturdy limbs they love to try Who Vlest can make the leather fly. Now winter's storms have passed away Flow cheering i 3 the brighter day ; The joyful birls in gladness sing, And budding flowers in beauty spring. New charms the lively fields can give. Where tennis bowls and cricket lire, Where youths with happy maidens gay In sunny hours delighted play. While cricket, England's noble game, Spreads th 6 bold batter's sportive fame Well pleased spectators love to see The ball hit strong and skilfully. When youth and manhood's prime is o'er, If games of strength can lure no more, On the smooth turf and velvet grass With bowls the pleasing hours we pass. Here free from every anxious care, Mid scenes so joyful, sweet and fair, From selfish strife we fain would rest With pleasure, peace and friendship blest. S. KENYON.

CAPITOLA THE MADCAP. BY MRS. E. D. E. N. S OUT HWOR TIL

CR APTER XXXl.—(Continued).

Late at night Mrs. Roche heard Colonel Le Noir come into the house and enter his chamber. Poor Clara slept no more that night; anxiety, despite of all her efforts, kept her wide awake, Yet, though anxious and wakeful, by prayer and endeavour she had brought her mind into a patient and submissive mood, so that when a servant knocked at her door in the morning with a message from Colonel Le Noir that She should be ready to set forth immediately after breakfast, she replied that she should obey him, and without delay she arose and commenced her toilet. All the family met for the last time around the board. The party was constrained. The meal was a gloomy one. On rising from the table Colonel Le Noir informed his ward that his travel- ling carriage was waiting, and that her baggage was already on, and requested her to put on her bonnet and mantle, and take leave of ,her servants. Clara turned to obey. Traverse went to her side, and whispered: "Take coura,e, dear love; my horse is saddled; I shall ride in attendance upon the carriage, whe- ther that man likes it os not; nor lose sight of you for one moment until we meet Williams with his hcche.zs corpus." "Nor even then„ dear Traverse! nor even then!. You will attend me to the court and be ready to take me back to this dear, dear home !" murmured Clara, in reply, "Yes, yes, dear girl. There, be, cheerful," whispered the young than, as he pressed her hand and released it. Colonel Le Noir had been a silent but frowning spectator of this little scene, and now that Clara was leaving the room, attended by Mrs. Rocke, he called the latter back, saying: " You will be so kind as to stop here a moment, Mrs. Rocke, and you also, young man." The mother and son paused to hear what he should have to say. "I believe it is the custom here, in discharging domestics, to give a month's warning or, in lieu of that, to pay a month's wages in advance. There, woman, is the money. You will oblige me by leav- ing the house to-day, togezher with your son and all your ether trumpery—as the premises are put in charge of an agent, who will be here this after- noon, clothed with authority to eject all loiterers and intruders." , While the colonel spoke, Marah Rocke gazed at him in a panic from which she seemed unable to rouse, herself, until Traverse gravely took her hand, saying: "My dear mother„ let me conduct you from the presence of this man, who does not know how to behave himself towards women. Leave me to talk with him, and do you, dear mother, go to Miss Day, who I know is waiting for you." Marah Rocke mechanically complied, and al- lowed Traverse to lead her from the room.

When he returned, he went up to Colonel Le Noir, and standing b efore him and looking him full and sternly in the'face, said, as sternly : "Colonel Le Noir, my mother will remain h,!re, and abide the decision of the Orphan's Court; until that has been pronounced, she does not sr at your or any man's bidding." " Villain ! out of my way 1" sneered Le Noir, endeavouring to pass him. Traverse prevented him, saying: "Sir, in consideration of your age, which should be venerable, your position which should pr,ve you honourable, and of this 'sacred house of mourning in which you stand, I have endeavoured to meet all the insults you have offered me with forbearance. But, sir, I am here to defend my mother's rights and to protect her from insult. ..9nd,l tell you plainly that you have atlronted her for the very last time. One word or lock of insult levelled at Marah Roche, and neither your age, position, nor this sacred roof shall protect you from personal chastisement at the hands of her son."

Le Noir, Who h9d listened in angry scorn, with many an ejaculation of contempt, now at the con- clusion which so galled his pride, broke out furi- ously, with: "Sir, you are a bully! If you were a gentleman I would call you out." "And I should not come if you did, sir. Duelling is nu-Christian, barbarous, and abominable in the sight of God and all good men. For thereat you may call me anything you please • but do not again insult my mother, for if you do, I shall hold it a ChristiJn duty to teach yon better manners," said Traverse, coelly' taking his hat and walking from the room. He mounted his horse, and stood ready to attend Clara to Staunton. '

Colonel Le Noir ground his teeth in impotent rage, muttei ing : "Take care, young man. I shall live to be re- venged upon you yet for these artronts !". And his dastard heart burned with the fiercer malignity that he had not dared to meet the eagle eye or encounter the strong arm of the upright and stalwart young man. Gnashing his teeth with ill-suppressed fury, he strode into the hall just as Mrs. • Rocke and Clara, in her ttaselling dress, descended the -stairs.

Clara threw her arms around Mrs. Rocke's neck, and weeping, said: " Good-bye I dear, best friend ! good-bye Heaven grant it. may not be for long. Oh, pray for me that I May be sent back to you !" "May the Lord have you. in His holy keeping, my child ! I shall pray until I hear from you I" said Marah, kissing and releasing her. Colonel Le Noir than took her by the hand, led her out, and put her into the oirriage. . Just before entering, Clara had turned to take a last look at her ld home; all, friends and servants, noticed the sorrowful, anxious, almost despairing look of her pale face, which seemed to ask : " Ah, shall I ever, ever return to you, dear old home, and dear, familiar friends ?" In another instant, she bad disikppeared within the carriage—which immediately rolled off. As the carriage was heavily laden, and the road Was in a very bad condition,. it was a full hour before they reached the town of Staunton. As the carriage drew up for a few moments before the door of the principal hotel,. and Colonel Le Noir was in the act of step,,ing out, a sherifrs officer, accompanied by Dr. Williams, approached, and served upon the Colonel a writ of habeas corpus, commanding him to bring his ward, Clara Day, into court. _ _ _

Colonel Le Noir laughed scornfully, saying: "And do any of you imagine this will serve your purposes? Ha! ha! the most hat it can do will be to delay my journey tor a few hours, until the decision of the judge, which will only serve to confirm my authority beyond all future possibility of questioning." "We will see that," said Dr. Willi Ams. "Drive to the court-house," ordered Colonel Le Noir.

And the carriage, attended by Traverse Rocks, Dr. Williams, and .the sheriff's officer, each on horseback, drove thither. And now, reader, I will not trouble you with detailed account of this trial. 'Clara, clothed in deep mourning, and looking pale and terrified, was led into the-court-room on the arm of her guardian. She was fotlowed closely by her friends, Traverse Rocke and Dr. Williams,: each of whom whispered encouraing words to the orphan. As the court had no pressing business on its hands, the case was immediately taken up, the will was read and attested by the attorney, who had drawn it up, and the, witnesses who had: signed it. Then the evidence of Dr. Williams amfDr. Roche was ta,kenconeerning the last verbal instruc- tions of the deceased. The ease occupied about th cc Lours, at the end of which.the judge gave a decision in favour of Colonel Le Noir. This judgnnnt ennled consternation to the heart of Clam and ali her friends. Clara lierself•:-ank rieLrly'fainting in the arms of her old friend, ti,.; venerable Dr. NVillia,ms. . • - Traverse, in biv ei*cess of spirit, approached and bent over her., . Colcuel Noll' spoke to Ile judge. " I deeply tlmt your hewr for the prompt hearing and equAy prompt decision of this case, and I will beg your I' to order the sheriff and his:officers to 5:.43 your judgment carried into effect, as I foresee vieknt opposition, 'and wish to prevent • trouble." " Cerrainly. Mr, Sheriff, you will see that Colonel Le Noir is .itt in possession' of his ward .and prAeeted in thy, riesht until e shall have placed her in securitv,". st'll.d the judge.. Clara; on hearing these words, lifted her head• from the old man's bosom, nerved her gentle heart; and in a clear, sweet, steaiy voice, said ; "It is needless precaution, your honour ; my fri,lnis are no law-breakers; and since the Court has given me into the custAy of-my guardian, I do not•dispute its judgment—l yield myself up to Colonel Le Noir." • "You do well, young lady;" said the judge.

"I am pleased, Miss Day, to see tnat you UnCler- stand and perform your duty; believe me, I shall do all that I can to make you happy," said Colonel Le Noir. Clara replied by a gentle nod; and then, with a slight blush mantling her pure cheeks, she ad- vanced a step, and placed herself immediately in front of the judge, saying; "But there is a word that I would speak to your honour." "Say on, young lady," said the judge. And as she stood there in her deep mourning dress, with her fair hair unbound and floating softly around her pale sweet face every eye in that court was spell-bound by her almost unearthly beauty. Before proceeding with what she was about to say she turned upon Traverse a look that brought him immediately to her side. "Your honour," she began, in a low, sweet, clear tone, "I owe it to Doctor Rocke here present, who has been sadly misrepresented to you, to say (what under less serious circumstances my girl's heart would shrink from avowing so publicly) that I am his betrothed wife—sacredly betrothed to him by almost the last act of my dear father's life. I hold this engagement to be so holy that no earthly tribunal can break or disturb it. And while I bend to your honour's decision, and yield myself to the custody of my legal guardian for the period of my minority, I here, declare to , all who may be in- terested that I hold my hand and heart irrevocably pledged to Doctor Rocke, and that, as his betrothed wife, I shall consider my self bound to correspond with him regularly, and to receive him as often as he shall seek my society, until my majority, when I and all I possess will become his own. And these words I force myself to speak, your honour, both in justice to my dear lost lather and his friend Traverse Rocke, and also to myself, that hereafter no one may venture to accuse me of clandestine proceedings, or distort my actions into impro- prieties, or in any manner call in question the con- duct of my father's daughter." And, with another gentle bow, Clara retired to the side of her old friend.

" You are likely to have a troublesome charge in your ward," said the sheriff apart to the colonel, who shrulged his, aioulders by way of reply. The heart of Traverse was torn by many conflict- ing passions, emotions and. impulses.; there was indignation at the decision of the court ; grief for the loss of Clara, and dread for her future!

One instant he felt a temptation to denounce the guardian as a villain and to charge the judge with being a corrupt politician, whose decisions were swayed by party interests. The next moment he f&t an impulse to catch Clara up in his arms, fight h's way through the crowd and carry her off. But all these wild emotions, passions and impulses he succeeded in controlling. ,Too we.' he knew that rage, do violence, or com- mit extravagance as he might, the law would take its course all tie same, While his heart NV as torn in this manner, Colonel Le Noir was urging the departure of his ward. And Clara came to her • lover's side and said, gravely and sweetly : "The law, you see, has decided against us, dear Traverse,! let us bend gracefully to a decree that we cannot annul; it cannot, at least, alter our sacred relation..., ; nor can anything on earth shake our stedfast faith in each other ; let us take comfort in that, and in the thought that the years will surely roll round at length and bring the time that shall re-unite us."

"Oh, my angel-girl I my angel-girl! your patient heroism puts me to the blush, for my heart is crushed in my bosom and my firmness quite gone !" said Traverse, in a broken voice,

_ , " You will gain -firmness, dear Traverse. Patient !' / patient! you should have heard me last night! I was so impatient that Doctor Wil- liams had to lecture me. But it would be strange if one did not learn something by suffering. / have been trying all night and day to school my heart to submission, and I hope I have succeeded, Tra- verse. Bless me and bid me good-bye." "The -Lord for ever bless and keep you, my own dear angel, Clara 1" burst from the lips of Traverze : "the Lord abundantly bless you 1" "And you 1" said Clara, "Good-bye ! good-bye 1" "Good-bye." And thus they parted. Clara was hurried away and put into the carriage by her guardian. Ah ! DO one but the Lord knew how much it had cost that poor girl to maintain her fortitude during that trying scene. She bad controlled herself for the sake of her, friends. But now, when she found herself in the carriage, her long strained nerves gave way —she sank exhausted and prostrated into the corner of her seat, in the utter collapse of woe.

_ But leaving the travellers to pursue their journey, we must go back to Traverse. Almost. broken-hearted. Traverse returned to Willow _Heights to convey the sad. tidings of his disappointment to his mother's ear. .Marah Rodze was so overwhelmed with grief at the news, that she was for several hours tncapable of action.

The arrival of the house agent was the first event that recalled her to her t,enses.

She aroused herself' to action, and assisted by Traverse -set to work to 'pack up her own and his wardrobe, and other personal effects. • And the next morning 3.larali Roche was re. established in her cottage. And the next week,- having equally divided their little capital, the mother aud son parted—Traverse, by her express desire, keeping to his original pla:# set out for the Far West. Yo be continued.)

tioniG oNE BETTER. —An American gentleman was recently travelling on the London and North Western Railway from Liverpool to Chester, via Runcorn. The stranger soon struck up a conversa- tion with an Englishman sitting opposite. "You travel pretty slick in the old country, mister," said the American. "Yes sometimes 70 miles an hour." "That's pretty well; but I reckon I've seen a train in New York State go so fast that the telegraph posts looked like the teeth of a fine comb?" "Indeed ! " rejoined the Englishman, evidently not caring to pursue the subject. "This . is Runcorn Bridge fine structure, isn't it?" "Good bridge—confounded bad smell!" said the Yankee, holdin,7 hi&nose, " Ah, it's the chemical works—but 100 l how the line curves—the train makes a half turn almost in its own length !" " Wal, sir; that may Dre sharp for the old country; but I tell you we have a curve on the Alleghany 'line where the guard at the end can light his pipe , at the engine tire as the train turns. That's so mister !"

A BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMBN

The information contained in this book ought to be known by every married woman, and it will not harfa the unmarried to read. The book is conveniently divided Into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty,or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out the best age Mr marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful in- formation that one can ordinarily get only from an in- telligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such marriages as s rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth chapter treats of mishaps and how to avoid them. The seventh chapter treats ct maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not due tolongings on the part of the mother, but rather to her poor health. The eighth' chapter teaches hos' to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made. comparatively easy if certain rules are obeyed; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is* up and about again. The eleventh ohapter treats of sterility ; gives the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result. The last chapter treats of the • "change," a most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book is written which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to married women. Some may think too much is told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means of attaining happiness. The ha* can be tied from Dr. 'X* R. ALLINSON, 25 Box. 4, Spanish-place Manchester-square, London, W., in return for a Postal order or is. id.

WARRINGTON SWIMMING CLUB.

THE POLO FIXTURES. There was a fair attendance at the annual general meeting of the members of the above-mentioned club on Tuesday evening, held at the Baths. Legh-street. Mr. C. Wood was voted to the chair. In the absence of the treasurer (Mr. H. Toft), Mr. D. Walsh read the state- ment of accounts. The total receipts amounted to £49 19s. Eld. After meeting all expenses there was a balance in hand of £2 7s. 4d., as compared with £7 2s. 10d. in hand at the commencement of the year. Mr. Walsh ex- plained that there had been a number of items which came under extraordinary expenditure, and which were not likely to occur this year.—The accounts were passed on the proposition of Mr. Scotson,seconded by Mr. Reid. —Mr. Critchley proposed several alterations to the rules. He suggested that the committee should consist of eight Instead of twelve members, and the ex-officio members. He found from experience that small committees worked best. No one supported the alteration, and it therefore fell to the ground. Mr. Critchley proposed, and it was agreed to,that the annuli Meeting of the club be held early in December,instead of in April. It was also de- cided, on Mr. Critchley's suggestion, that seven days' notice of motion be given to the secretary, instead of fourteen as hitherto. The election of officers was then ; er proceeded with, and resulted as follows :—Councillor Percy Davies. president Mr. Robert Pi ntM .P" Councillor Roberts,Mr. Thomas Morris. Mr. John Hough- ton, Mr. Luke Talbot (chief constable),with poi the mem- bers of the Baths' Committee. vice-presidents; Mr. D. Walsh, secretary ; Mr. Wright, assistant secre- tary; Mr. H. Toft, treasurer; Mr. T. Locker, captain; and Mr. Scotson, sub-captain. The fol- lowing members were appointed on the committee on the motion of Mr. Sherlock,seconded by Mr. Scotson: Messrs. Critchley, Grounds, Reid, Wilson, Garaide, Bea- man, Creighton, W. Ashton, Robertson, Wood, Harding and Hatton. Mr. Grounds was appointed a delegate to the Liverpool and District Polo Association, and Mr. Locker to the Northern Counties Association.—The Chairman said the rules of the club,Nvere in a very crude form, and did not meet their requirements. He thought a sub-committee should be appointed to revise the rules. This was agreed to, and the following were appointed a sub-committee for the purpose :—Messrs. MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL BILL.

THE PROPOSED ABOLITION OF THE TIDAL OPENINGS. The Bill promoted by the Manchester Ship Canal COM- pany for the purpose, inter alia, of obtaining power to close the tidal openings into the Mersey, on Tuesday came before a Select Committee of the House ef Com- mons, of which Mr. Blake is chairman. The promoters were represented by Mr. Pember, Q.C. Mr. Fletcher Moulton, Q.C., Mr. Balfour Browne. Q.C., and Mr. Gra- ham. The petitioners against the Bill included the Cor- poration of Liverpoel, represented by Mr. Pope, Q C.; the Mersey Docks and Harbour Board, represented by Mr. Pope, Q.C., Mr. Worsley Taylor, Q.C., Mr.Squarey, and Mr. T. R. Hughes ; the London and North-Western Railway, represented by Mr. Pope, Q.C., Mr. Littler, Q C., and Mr. Moon; the Weaver Navigation Trustees, represented by Mr. Pope, Q.C., and Mr. Moon ; the Cor- poration of Warrington' represented by Mr. Pembroke Stephens, Q.C.; the Salt Union, Limited, represented ley Mr. Freeman, Q.C.; the Corporation of Widnes, the United Alkali Company, and others, represented by Mr. Freeman, Q.C., and Mr. S warey ; Brunner, Mond, and Co. represented by Mr. Worsley Taylor, Q.C., and Mr. H. Parker Lowe; the Rural District Council of Runcorn represented by Mr. E. Pollock, Q.C., and Mr. H. Lloyd ; the Cheshire County Council, repre- sented by Mr.ll. Lloyd ; the Shropshire Union Railways and Canal Company, represented by Mr. Littler, Q.C., and Mr. Moon ; Corporation of Birkenhead, represented by Mr. Squarey ; the Marquis of Cholmondeley, and others, represented by Mr. Reader Harris, Q.C. ; the trustees of the Ate Mr. E. W. Park-Yates,represented by Sir W. T. Marriott, Q.C. ; while there were also several other private petitioners against the Bill, for whom counsel including Mr. Blennerhassett, Q.C., Mr. Shaw, and others appeared. Mr. Pember said that this was a Bill with various ob- jects,and he would first take that which concerned the greater number of opponents and ask for a decision upon it. That part was the proposal to close certain tidal openings in the embankment of the canal on the side of the estuary of the Mersey. The canal was tidal from Latchford Locks to Eastham, a distance of 21 miles. At the time cf the inception ot the canal it was not thoroughly understood what effect its making would have upon the Mersey,and these openings were provided. on the supposition that the estuary would be benefited It was also thought that they would be innocuous to the canal, but experience—and that was his case—had shown that they were not necessary for the benefit of the estuary, and that they were very harmful to the canal in several ways. In the first place they created cross-currents in the canal, this tidal water pouring in at right angles to the longitudinal current. These cross- currents were most inconvenient, to say the least. Secondly, these openings let in an enormous amount of silt, with which the waters of the Mersey were heavily charged. To enab.athem to judge of the amount of the silt he might state that in the course of the three years since the opening of tha banal no less than 1,9b0,000 cubic yards of silt had been dredged out of the canal from the tidal portion. In the third place, a very great expense in dredging was consequently incurred. Not only was there the expense, but as the deposit took place very rapidly it decreased the available depth for the purposes of navigation before it could be removed, so much so that instead of there being the full depth of 26ft. it was often two or three feet less. The result was that the repute of the canal as a highway for the largest sea-going traders was considerably impaired. More than that, the canal was cumbered throughout its ttdal length ' with the necessary dredging plant. There was one other item which he must not forget, and that was the irrup- tion of floe ice in the winter time. There had been one severe winter—that of 189—since the canal was opened and there was an immense amount of such ice carried from the estuary of the Mersey into the canal, causing a very serious drawback to the navigation. Mr. Pember proceeded to contend that the closing of the tidal open- ings as proposed would restore some six or seven million cubic yards of water to the estuary, and said that when the works were completed the only influx or efflux of tidal water would be through the sluices. The Chairman: Two-thirds of the water which you acknowledge to be abstracted at present will be left to the estuary by the closing of the tidal openings? Mr. Pember replied that that was so. He added that one of the towns most interested in the upper regiod of the Mersey was Warrington, and Warrington was so thoroughly satisfied as to the harmlessness of the change proposed that she did not oppose the bill, in spite of the fact that she had an engineer so eminent as Mr. Wolff Barry. Mr. Pembroke Stephens intimated that this statement was not quite accurate, though Warrington had every hope of being able to agree. Mr. Pember: I understood an agreement had been arrived at. Mr. Pembroke Stephens : On certain points. Dealing with opinions against the bill Mr. Pember said that some petitioners expressed the fear that the Company might intend to raise the water level in the canal, with the view of saving dredging, but that was a very " fine " fear. The limitation of the headway of ships by tbe railway bridges was the safeguard for these petitioners. The Company could not afford to lose that headway. What the Company hoped to achive by the closing cf the .tidal openings was a reduction of the dredging, the necessity for which arose in the main from silt brought in by the tide. Certain landowners represented that their lands were just above the level of the water in the canal, and that at certain times their drains were waterlocked by the water of the canal being above the ordinary level, and they feared that any de- lay of the going down of the ebb tide would increase the period during which the draies were locked. The Company, however,proposed to take means to remove the possibility of damage in consequence of the retarda- tion of the ebb. It was also suggested by petitioners that the closing of the tidal openings might cause the canal water to become more stagnant, and interfere with the health of those who lived on the banks of the canal. But as a matter of fact the population along the banks was very sparse, and the towns which had been the worst offenders ih discharging sewage into the canal were gradually setting their houses in order, the conse- quence being that the condition of the water was im- proving month by month. A vast amount of objection- able matter entered the canal from the estuary through the tidal openings,and the closing of the openings would keep that out in future. Evidence for the promoters was theficalled. Mr. J. K. Bythell, chairman ot the Ship Canal Com- pany, examined by Mr. Moulton, Q.C., said owners of vessels objected to allow their ships to pass the tidal openings while the water was flowing through, because of the cross current, and frequently a large steamer would lose a day in the voyage from Manchester to Liverpool in consequence of having to be tied up. The inflow of silt from the estuary was so great that after a storm there would be a deposit of two or three feet, and vessels of a deep draft were unable to continue their journey. The Company had eight large dredgers, two sea-going hoppers, a dozen flats, and a great many barges at work. There was no doubt whatever that the drainage from the upper reaches caused a deposit in the estuary which was washed through the tidal openings into the canal. During severe winter weather no diffi- culty had been caused by ice forming in the canal. The difficulty was occasioned by the large amount of floe ice which came through the tidal openings. As to the height of the water, during the ebb tide they proposed to always lower it te the normal height. Cross-examined by Mr. Reader Heins, Q.C.,_ witness said he was not aware that the Ship Canal Company were bound by any Act to have any particular headway. In reply to Mr. Freeman, Q.C., he said an uninter- rupted watercourse was very important to Widnes, be- cause ,of its large trade with Liverpool, Northwich, Winsford, and other places. Widnes had objected to the proposal of the Canal Company to make certain open- ings near the lock there, because it would interfere with the access to the Widnes Docks, and a tidal opening was fixed in the position suggested by Widnes. That was one of the openings the Ship Canal Company were pro- posing to close. More silt came through this opening than through any of the others. Mr. Pope: Your case is based upon this. These open- ings injure the canal, and they do not benefit the estu- ary ?—Yes, and further, that they diminish the flow in the upper part of the estuary. Are you content to try the question upon that issue that these tidal openings injure the canal, and do not benefit the Mersey ?—There are other grounds. We say that we shall benefit the other parts of the river by res- toring water now taken by the canal. The Chairman: I think you may assume that we are not going to destroy the Mersey estuary. Mr. Pope: It has never been asserted that the con- etruction of the canal would destroy the estuary of the Mersey. Mr. Pember : In 1885 Mr. Steveven said it would be covered with arass.—(Laughter.) Mr. Pope: r•That was the upper portion. What is the cost of dredging ?—We spend at the rate of £50,000 a year on dredging, and the greater part was spent on the tidal portion of the canal. The Committee at the close of Mr. Bythell's evidence adjourned.

The consideration of the bill, was resumed on Wednes- day by a committee of the House of Commons, consist- ing of Mr. Blake (chairman), Mr. Fardell, Mr. Samuel (Stockton-on-Tees), and Mr. J. Heywood Johnstone. Mr. W. 11. Hunter, engineer to the company, was of opinion that the diversion of the estuary water into the canal by the tidal openings had somewhat reduced the level of high water at Warrington, and to a lesser degree at Runcorn. The estuary of the Mersey above the nar- rows was encumbered by banks of silt, alluvial, and sew- age, largely formed from the waste discharged by adjoin- ing- pits. The sewage also came from Liverpool (which had no system of treating its sewage), Garston, Widnes, Warrington, Runcorn, and Birkenhead. He estimated the quantity of untreated swage discharged from these places into the estuary at 340,000 tons a year. Even where the banks appeared to be sand, there was found to be sewage below. The canal was practically a catch. pit for the refuse that was carried into it through the tidal openings. Dredging was a source of enormous expense, and emp'oyed 30 vessels. Shoals of mud formed with incredible rapidity, so that while one week

HOW A BUDGET SPEECH IS REPORTED.

In the "Windsor Magazine" for April, a brightly written article, by John Rendle, gives an account of the "Budget Night in the House of Commons from a press- man's point of view : " If you ask the gentlemen in the Press Gallery," says the writer, "you will find that, though they deal with whltever happens in Parliament in a cheery and unob- trusive bid confident manner, they have very strong convictions about Parliamentary oratory. Chancellors may hum' and ha,' and yes ' and ' no,' get into polemical quagmires, stick in the fiscal mud, and indeed commit any Parliamentary atrocity of which a human being can be capable, yet our friends in the Gallery are ' never flurried or flustered or lose that quiet confidence so necessary for Press work in Parliament. These big nights in the Press Gallery would never go smoothly if intellectual capacity were not commandingly superior to both the quality and quantity of the raw material to be dealt with. To struggle with a mass of shorthand notes full, it may be, of gaps through non-hearing and wrong words,threughmiss hearing,figures muddled up,sentences that will not read,statements that are directly opposed to statements in another part of the Speech, in order that when it is presented to the public, it shall read gram- matically and intelligibly, and be understood by the ordinary newspaper reader, if he should wish to read it, is literary work, demanding skill and discretion, which is really the Measure of the capacity of the Press repre- sentatives in Parliament to cope with the great and im- portant questions that necessarily arise. On these big nights, when the various reporting corps are in their full strength, when leader-writers and summary-writers eagerly devour the Chancellor's proposals and the special points of the debate that follows; when each proposal,as it comes hot from the Minister's lips, is immediately put on the wires in the telegraph room and sent to all parts of the kingdom and the world for the use of news- papers and individuals ; hen the writing rooms, glow- ing under the electric light, are filled with busy Press- men engaged in making their reports ; when sketch writers are describing the scene in the House and London letter 'writers are dealing with the latest tips from the Lobby; when messengers are here, there, and everywhere, collecting 'copy' for the London as well as for he Provincial Press ; when the well-known click of the telegraph instruments is almost unceasingly heard the sitting through—here you find work being done quietly, but surely and effectively, requiring diligence, acuteness, knowledge and rapidity."
