NOTES AND NEWS.

M. Jules Simon tells rather a pretty story in the "Revue de Paris," and as the annals of the Franco-Ger- man war afford but few examples of similar episodes is quite worth zepeating. The late maestro Ambroise Thomas remained in Paris throughout the Beige and the Commune. As soon as things had quieted down, his first care was to visit his charming suburban villa at Argenteuil, expectiqg to find a mere heap of ruins, for that quarter had suffered much at the hands of the in- vadors. To his intense surprise he found the ornamen- tal iron gate close&the garden well tended and trim, the home itself in perfect order. In a stateof bewilderment he opened the front door and entered !the dining-room, where a clue to the mystery met his byes. On the table lay a card bearing the name of his unwelcome tenant, a German officer of distinction,underneath whose military title were pencilled the words," Meyerbeer's nephew.'

Parisians are showing much onterprise in introducing motor cars into their streets. There are a great number of these horseless vehicles in Paris, and as they run along at a greater speed than the ordinary carriages they add considerably to the dangers of the road,already sufficiently great. Most of the vehicles are constructed after the model of a large bath-chair, and seat only two or three people at the outside.

William Rontgen, who has leaped to fame so decidedly in connection with the'much-discussed X rays, has just turned his half-century of years. He has been a profes- sor in the Wurzburg University since 1888, and for the nine years previous was assistant to Professor Sundt. The Usiversity of Zurich had the distinction of educating

Apropos of the multitude of divorces and second and third marriages that have taken place in the section of American society that seethes round the Vandertilts,and the latest excitement, the marriage of Mrs. W. B. Van- derbilt to Mr. Belmont, the following little tale says "The Man of the World," is not amiss :—" At a childs' party little Miss Belmont, the daughter of Mrs. George Rives, once Mrs. 0. H. P. Belmont, found herself danc- ing in the same set with the youngest Vanderbilt boy. With perfect New York fin de siècle nonchalance she said to him: ' I hear that you have a new papa at your house. How do you like him? He was my papa once I'"

Bismarck hates being stared at. "it is not very pleasant to have an opera-glass levelled at_you at four- teen paces or a revolver at four," he says. But he does not object to interviewers. " I refused myself to three diplomats, he said one day, "but I received four journ- alists. I learned more from the than I should have done from the others."

Aristocracies in different places and ages have prided themselves on many different things. One of the queer- est, perhaps, is the laristocracy according to cheeses, which, according to the " Neue Zuricher Zeitung," pre- vails among the patricians of Zermatt. . The aristocracy of families is valued by the number and age of the cheeses they possess. There are families who possess cheeses made at the time of the French Revolution. When a child is born, a cheese is manufactured,which is then called by the name of the child. It is partly eaten when its namesake gets married, each wedding guest , tasting a portion. The cheese is then put away again, and finally cut into and finishei at the funeral of the person whose name it bears. When a young man woos a maiden he begs to be allowed to dine with her family on a Sunday: His offer being accepted, the lovers wait anxiously to see whether the girl's father will cause the cheese to be set on the table. At the end of the long meal, if all goes well, the master of the house solemnly fetches the cheese bearing the would-be bride's name,sets it on the table, cuts it, and gives a piece to the young couple. When they have eaten it they are a betrothed pair. The others at the table partake of the cheese and drink to the eternal friendship of the two families.

The porpoise which early last week went up the Thames as far as Twickenham has, thanks to the "sportsmen "who hunted it, met with a wretched death. The animal was discovered stranded on the mud at Chiswick bleeding from the mouth and back,and died shortly after being found. On examination there were found several harpoon wounds and a deep hole which appeared to have been inflicted by a bullet,while below the dorsal fin there was a large deep gash which had been caused by the propeller of some tug. The poor hunted-tn-death mammal, which died from its in- pries, was a very fine specimen of the porpoise, weigh- ing nearly 9001 b., its measurement being—Length lift. 3in.; girth, sft: ; and width across tail (which was beau- tifully shaped), 2ft. 41n.

It has been decided that the challenge cup which the Commander-in-Chief has recently given for a competi- tion in riding and shooting by cyclist sections of Volun- teer corps shall be contested for the first time at Risley on Saturday, May 2. Each cyclist team is to consist ot eight men under an officer. They will start from Thames Ditton, and fellow routes which will make them cover forty-four miles in getting to the firing-point. ,the ride being in marching order and military formation. At the range they will fire ten volleys at 600 yards and 500, after which each man will fire five rounds independently. The rules have been drawn up by a committee of Regu- lar and Volunteer officers who are themselves cyclists and have given much attention to the adapt‘tion of cycles to military purposes, Major General Strawy being at their head.

A telegram from Italy conveys a piece of curious and rather ghastly information. In response to a demand of General Baldissera, three" experienced mechanics" are about to leave for Massowah to arrange artificial legs for the soldiers and Ascari in Africa who have lost their limbs. One hundred artificial legs have already been forwarded to Africa. Nearly all those who have suf- fered amputation are Ascari or natives, who were made prisoners at the battle of Adua, and who were.mutilated by the Shoens.

Considerable sensation has been occasioned in Ameri- can scientific circles by a claim put forward on behalf of Professors Pratt, Wrightman and Bennett, of the Medical College, Cioicago, to the effect that they have discovered that Rontgen rays are able to kill the bacteria of cholera, diphtheria, pneumonia, influenza, glanders, typhoid, tuberculosis and anthrax.

A plan of separating the sexes during public worship, I tried in a Bristol church, has ended in the appearance of several people before the local magistrates. Sum- monses for assault were heard on Monday against two churchwardens of St. Simon's,in that city,narnely, Win. Stagg and William Frost, the complainant being a parishioner, named Samuel Blower. A number of parishioners opposed the separation of the sexes, and in- sisted on sitting with thrir families. Complainant was one of these, and there were difficulties on successive Sundays, culminating in a scene at the evening service of Sunday week, when,it was alleged, an attempt was made to eject Blower from the women's side of the church, where he was with his wife and daughter. He said that when he declined to move Stagg tried to pull him from the pow,but he held to the benches. Witnesses calculated that the struggle lasted from seven to fifteen minutes,and con'sternation was caused amongst the con- gregation. Complainant, was mistaken about Frost, whom he had confused with another man,who was called as a witness,and the summons against Frost was dis- missed. Counsel deniod that Stagg had used any violence, and claimed for the churchwardens the power of constables under the Brawling Act. It was contended for the prosecution that the discretionary power ot Stagg had been exceeded, and the magistrates regarded the legal points involved as so important that they com- mitted Stagg for trial at the Quarter Sessions in order to get the decision of the Recorder.

In 1893 three men who have since become world famous, were at the University of Strasburg, although they were then unknown to one another or to fame. They were Paderewski, Röntgen and Tesla. Paderewski, was at that time an instrdctor in music in the Univer- sity; Röntgen was a professor of physics, and Tesla was installing the electric light plant in the University. Of the three men, Rontgen was the last to achieve fame. When his X-ray discovery was announced, the recognition of his work was prompt, and now he is Baron Röntgen. Tesla has declined several offers of titles and decorations made to him by rulers of foreign countries, but takes a proper pride in the honours con- ferred upon him by scientific bodies in different parts of the world ; and the whole world of art delights in hon- ouring Paderewski.

The "St. James's Gazette" says :—A member of a well-known shipping firm writes to us to say that it is within his knowledge that German coal is actually being shipped for England. This further instance of the pro- gress which Germany is making in competing with this country is certainly rather startling. Our correspon- dent has it on the best authority that charters are being concluded to bring Westphalian coal from Duisburg, Hoetaelt, or Ruhrort-on-the-Rhine to London. The answers to the question how such competition can be explained is very simple. The German State railways carry coal for export at a nominal rate, and the carriage thus saved acts exactly as a bounty on export.

There was a large attendance of borough magistrates at the Preston Police Court on Wednesday, when the Mayor presided. The general public also mustered in strong force, presumably owing to a charge of assault in which some extraordinary allegations were made. The defendant is a boy named Herbert Berry, 14 years old, described as a pupil teacher at St. Mary's Church Schools, and he is charged with assaulting three of the scholars, named James Scanlon, Alexander Cardwell, and John Billington. The allegations are said to be that Berry gave each of the scholars named the alternative of having a tooth drawn or being caned for misconduct, and sound teeth are said to have been drawn from their gums. On the case being called, Mr. W. Blackhurst, for the defendant, asked thtt it might be adjourned, as it would be necessary to call a number of witnesses who were not available. After some discussion the hearing was adjourned until Tuesday next.

I FRE C i SALVATIONIST

TROUBLE.

The Salvation Army is making itself known in France. A well-known medical man of Paris, Dr. Thierry Mieg, decorated with the Legion of Honour, had a startling experience the other day when ,a lady of the French " Army " made her way to his surgery. Exhausted by marching, hard work, cold, damp, and privation—in a terrible condition of nerveus breakdown —this girl (for she was little more) had actually to be supported in the waiting room by two or three of the hundreds who throng thelamous doctor's clinic. France, which is behind England in every other respect, honour- ably takes the lead in one matter. French physicians will prescribe whatever remedy will cure their patient. In this case, Dr. Thierry Mieg at once prescribed Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. In his own words this treatment " wrought a miracle."

"Dear Doctor," . the lady wrote (of course in French), only a few days afterwards, "thanks for the second box of Pills which!! y.su have sent me. I cannot sufficiently thank God for the good that has been procured to me by Dr. Williams' Pills, on which I am sure He has cast His benediction. lam not the same person I was a few months ago. I could never have done the work which I am to-day able to accomplish. I cannot tell you how much they have done for me. I suffered in the head,in the limbs,and especially with ex- treme nervousness. Under the influence of the Pills you gave me I have been able to march,to work hard, and to assist every evening at the services of the 'Army. without

fatigue. "For yourself Amy dear doctor, I pray that heaven may richly bless you fcr all that you have done for me and my sisters of the Salvation Army.—Ensign Bon-

jour."- Dr. Williams' Pink Pills cure not only in cases of nervous breakdown, bat all the many disorders which arise from an impoverished state of the blood and nerves, such as anaemia, pale and sallow complexion, general muscular weakness,loss of appetite, shortness of breath, pains in the back,nervous headache,early decay, all form of female weakness,hysteria, paralysis,locomo- tor ataxy, sciatica, palpitations,low vitality,and wasting strength from any cause. These Pills are genuine only with the full name, Dr. NI illiams' Pink Pills for Pale People, and are sold by chemists and by Dr. Williams, Medicine Company, 46, Holborn-viaduct, London, E.C.. at 23. 9d. a box,or six for 133. 9d. They are unrivalled as a tonic and strengthening medicine for both sexes.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday Mr. BRODRICK questioned by Mr. P. O'Brien as to the disposal of the £l,BOO which had been put on the Army Estimates as an extra allowance to the Duke of Cambridge, said the vote would simply be withdrawn. Mr. LONG, replying to Mr. Price, said he had received a resolution passed by the Canadian House of Commons protesting against the permanent exclusion of Canadian cattle on the ground of the existence of pleuro-pneu- monia in Canada,but,while regretting that on any ques- tion they should find themselves unable to accede to the wishes of the Canadian Government, Her Majesty's Government felt that it was not possible, consistently with the interests of agriculture at home, to postpone legislation on the subject until further experiments had deciced the point at issue. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN informed Mr. G. Wyndham that he was unable to say when he would be able to lay addi- tional papers with regard to South African affairs on the table, but be would do so as soon as possible. Mean- while all rumours coming from South Africa should be received with the greatest caution. Asked by Sir E. A. BARTLETT whether, in view of the important colonial and Imperial interests now at stake in South Africa, the Government would, at once reinforce the Imperial troops there, Mr. Chamberlain said Sir H. Robinson had offered, after consultation with the general officer commanding the troops at the Cape and hearing from Messrs. Rhodes,a.nd Duncan and Lord Grey, to send up at once to the disturbed districts 300 cavalry and 200 mounted infantry from Natal, in addi- tion to the volunteers and police already arranged for. He would also raise a force of 200 Basutos to aid irr quel- ling the insurrection. In reply to Mr. Dalziel, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN said that under existing arrangements he should expect the British South African Company to pay the expense. Mr. G. BALFOUR, in asking leave to introduce a bill to further amend the' law relating to the occupation and ownership of land in Ireland," explained that it was the policy of the Government to stimulate the pur- chase of holdings by cultivators, but he considered it would be impossible to proceed on compul- sory lines, for to do that would involve an expenditure of over £100,000,000, even if it did not ex.tend to £200,000.000 or more. Far the 'present they must be content to proceed gradually. They must try to oil the machinery', and the bill contained amendments of pro- cedure in-tended to obviate delay and diminish expense. They also proposed•to promote purchase by making the terms of repayment easier,by abolishing the purchase of insurance, and by diverting the county percentage to the sinking fund. As to the evicted tenants, they were prepared cnce more to re-enact the thirteenth clause of the Act of Is9l for a further period of twelve months, and to empswer the Land Commission to act as mediat- ors between the parties. After some remarks by Mr. JOHN MORLEY and Mr. DimoN the bill was brought in and read a first time. The second reading was fixed for the 27th inst.

TUESDAY. In the Hcuse of Commons on Tuesday, on the motion ,or the sef.lond reading of a Bill promoted by the London and North-Western Railway Company for the purpose of enabling them to widen the line between Chester and Holyhead, Mr. Lloyd-George and Mr. H. Lewis com- plained of the high-rates charged for goods on that por- tion of the Company's syst.m. Mr. FIELD also com- plained of the extremely high rates charged for passe '- gers between Dublin and Euston. Sir W. H. HOULDS- WORTH submitted that the Company did all they could to encourage and foster traffic, and that the question of passenger rates was most carefully considered. Mr. Louon moved the rejection of the bill, and after further debate Sir W. H. Houtmsworern moved the closure. The closure was carried by 221 voteg to 107; Mr. Lough's amendment was rejected by 207 votes to 130: and tho second reading was carried by 203 votes to 124. Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE afterwards moved that the vote of Sir W. H. flouldsworth should be disallowed; on the ground that be was financially interested in the London and North-Westorn Railway Company. Mr. 1. seconded the motion, which was eventually withdrawn on Mr. A. J. BADFOUR assenting to the appointment of a Committee to consider the whole matter. In reply to Mr. Buchanan ,Lord G. HAMILTON said that at present he believed the Egyptian army would prove adequate to the task in hand, and the Government had therefore no intention of bringing troops from India to join the expedition up the Nile V Otey. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN informed Mr. Labouchere that up to the present he had no reason to believe that the rising in Matabeleland was due to any mismanage- ment on the part of the Chartered Company. Mr. CuazoN informed Mr. Dalziel that there was no foundation for the report that the 9th Lancers were under orders to proceed to the Soudan. Asked by Mr. DALZIEL as to a speech made recently by Sir Redvers Buller, the Adjutant General, at the Cre- diton Constitutional Club, Mr. Brodrick said the speech did not appear to contravene the Queen's Regulations. It seemed to him to be of a military rather than a party character. The Secretary for War held that officers on the headquarters staff should lie soecially careful not to become involved in political vestilns. Mr. A. J. BALFOUR stated that the second reading of the Education Bill would not be moved before Thursday week, and that there were certain bills that must have precedence over the Irish Land Bill, but he could not now state what they were WEDNESDAY. In the House of Commons on Wednesday, on the order for the second reading of the Local Government (Ireland) Provisional Order (N). 2) Bill, providing for the transfer from the Grand Jury of the county of Armagh to the Town Commissioners of the city of Armagh of certam powers and responsibilities with re- spect to roads and bridges, th- qoestion of the represen- tation of the Roman Catholics of Armagh on the Town Commissioners was raised. Mr. T. M. Healy said that though the majority of the neople of Armagh were Roman Catholics,not a single Town Commissioner was a Roman Catholic. The second reading was carried by 120 votes to 75. Dr. TANNER moved the second reading of a Bill to amend the constitution of hoards of guardians in Ire- land, and to extend their powers under the Labourers Acts. Mr. Davitt seconded the motion. Mr. G.Balfour said the Government believed that the present system of local government in Ireland was in every way unsatis- factory, and that it required reform in the direction of increasing popular repsesentation. With regard to the present Bill, however, they were not able to consent to the second reading in the sense that by doing so they accepted the principles and proposals in it. The second leading was carried nem con., but a proposal to refer the measure to the Standine Committee on Law was defeated by 207 votes to 154. and the House shortly afterwards aej )urned.

SPREAD OF MORMONISM.

Thousands of converts to the Mormon faith are reported in Kentuckyeand a correspondent of the" New York Recorder," writing from Pointsville, says :—The Mormon Church has evidently determined to capture the Big Sandy Valley for the religion of Joe Smith. The missionaries swarm from the head of Tug and Livesay and Panther and Beaveir almost to Catlettsburg. There are missionaries in a score of other territories, and over in West Virginia there are half a dozen churches and a membership of several hundred. All the missionaries in Kentucky (eighty-six is the estimated number) are men of fine appearance, well educated, shrewd, in love with their doctrines, enthusiastic, and immensely popular with the women. The Kentucky Mormon preachers here are under the auspices of the " Sou•hern Missions,' whose headquarters are at Salt Lake. The men have an abundance of Money, and are well dressed. E :stern Kentucky is being flooded with Mormon tracts, which state the doctrines and practic s of the Church of the Latter-day Saints. They are diligently and thoroughly circulated by the elders, and are making a strong impression. These elders preach in every neighbour- hood, and large throngs attend the services, which are held in barns or schoolhouses, because the churches are denied to them. The Mormon creed contains thir- teen articles of faith, everyone of them made up from the doctrines of some other Church. There are MOl- - elders in at Last twenty-six counties. There are always two of them together, sometimes four. At least 1,000 persons are at heart converted to the Joe Smith religion. In Johnson and Martin ceunties fifty families are going to Utah. All who have not the means are not only taken to Utah, but are furnished with a farm and enough provisions and cash to support them until they can raise a crop. Elder White says "It is against the rules to tell about our financial affairs, but I do not be- - lieve I sin greatly when I say to you that the Mormon Church is as great a missionary organisation, so far as its members and wealth are concerned, as was ever the Methodist Church famous for its missionary spirit. We have 1,000,000 defiers surplus in our missionary fund, and our elders are at work in West Virginia, Kentucky, Tennessee, Arkansas, and a few in Alabama and Georgia. They are no- longer polygamists, because of obedi- ence to the laws; but it is perhape true that as a class we see no sin in polygamy. The Mormons Who go to Mexico practise polygamy or n t, as they please. The Church owns vast tracts of land there, and several very valuable silver and 'gold mines. We are growing very rapidly, and Mermen churches are soon to be built in several cities,among them New York and Chicago." It is diffieult to prophesy what will be the final result of this ,Mormon invasion in Kentucky. The rural newspapers are becoming very outspoken in their strictures on the elders, and do not hesitate to ad-- vise the severest measures to drive them out of the State. They are freely charged with breaking up fami- lies, creating neighbourhood disturbances, and using all their persuavive talents to induce women and girls to go to Utah. The elders are very sly. They talk soberly in public and in their‘sermons,but they go into houses and resort to peculiar methods to ve,n the women for Utah. There have been no seandals of a wrsonal character. Tne elders understand that a lynching would follow, and they are circumspect ; but there is a general de- mand among the better classes that a halt be called. R. C.Evans,of Johnson County,says : " Nearly 100 Mormon elders are proselytising in the State; scores of families are being dismembered; youeg women in droves are sent to Utah,even wives and mothers an, sometimes re- fusing to take their children. It is time for these things to stop. It is a disgrace to the State. It is true the majority who favour the Church of Joe Smith are of the ignorant class, but that does not justify their being de- ceived."

SHIP CANAL

THE PORT OF MSNOHEsTER.

During the fortnight ended April 10,251 seagoing cargo vessels, with an aggregate net registered tonnage of 80,525 toos.arrived at and sailed from the various docks. wharves. 8/3., on the Ship Canal. Of this total 43,423 tons entered from or cleared fpr foreign ports. Thirty.- seven vessels arrived with general cargoes, 19 with stone and slate, 18 with pottery clay and flints, 45 with pig iron, six with green fruit, lour with cotton, four witla iron ore,two with woed pulp, one with steel billets, one with pig lead,one with copperas, and one with timber. A number of Irish cattle and horses were landed by the steamers of the new Dublin and Manchester service. Amongst the cargoes exported were 41 of general mer- chandise—including two to Bombay and one to Aus- tralia—salt was shipped to St. John,New Brunswick,and to various home ports, and a number .of coal cargoes' were takers outwards by coasting vessels.

TdE EXA_MEINER.

SATURDAY, APRIL 18th, 1896.

A question of very great import- Private Interests. Public versus ance to the country was raised in the House of Commons on Tuesday, and raised in such a way. too, that some good results may be expected to follow. The more or less open advocacy by the directors of companies who have seats in Parliament of the private interests with which they are connected has often been commented upon, but not much has been done to put a stop to it. It is said that between six and seven hundred different director- ates are represented in the House of Commons. This does not, of course, mean that practically every member of the Commons is on a board of directors of one kind or another, because in some instances one member represents many director- ates. It is said, for instance, that seven Tory members hold between them no fewer than 136 directorships, and that two Liberal Unionist members monopolise twenty-seven. J t is well-known that the present Government fights tooth and nail in favour of the water mono- polists of London and against that popularly- elected body the London County Council, and in this connection it has been mentioned that two members of the Government are directors of Lon- don water companies. We do not believe that the action of the Government on the water bills was influenced by these two members, or that the two members, whoever they may be, ever attempted to use their influence in favour of the companies of which they are directors, but still it is easy to see that as long as these connections last there may at any time be a conflict in the minds of legislators between private and public interests On Tuesday the subject was raised in a very pointed form. The London and North-Western Railway Company are promoting a Bill in Parlia- ment for the widening of the railway between Chester and Holyhead, and on Tuesday the Bill came up for its second reading. The methods of the great railway company were severely crit'cised by both the Irish and the Welsh members. The Company has, practically,- a monopoly of Irish traffic, and the Irish members complained that it takes advantage of this to im- pose high charges both for passengers and for goods. It was said that many facilities which are accorded to Scotchmen ate withheld from Irish- men, simply because there is competition with lother railways for Scotch business. Welshmen also have certain grievances against the Londoit and North-Western, and consequently the repre- sentatives of what Tories sometimes describe as the "Celtic fringe" combined to make a rather formidable opposition to the Bill. The interests of the railway company were championed by Sir W. H. HOULDSWORTH a gentleman who has a' high reputation in the commercial world. When his vote was challenged later on by Mr. LLOYD GEORGE, Sir WILLIAM HOULDSWORTH candidly admitted that he is a director of the London and North Western Railway Company, and e- clared that he was "bound to do his duty" by get- ting the Bill passed. This frank declaration raised the question of the responsibilites of members of Parliament in its most naked ,form, and a signifi- cant discussion followed. Mr. BALFOUR blundered badly whenlas leader of the House he endeavoured to prove that no principle was involved, though he certainly won a sympathetic cheer when he de- clared that he had never served as a director. The true bearings of the case were more accurately taken by Mr. HALDANE, Mr. LOUGH and Mr. J. BURNS. Mr. HALDANE pointed out that members wereinot sent tothe House of Commons to promote bills in the interest of Corporations of which they were members or shareholders but to give votes as members of the House in the public interest. Mr. LOUGH bluntly asserted that "there was too much of the director business" in the House of Commons, and Mr. BURNS was even more out- spoken when he declared that "the suspicion had grown that in recent years the House had become too much the rendezvous of guinea pigs' and promoters ofj railways, gas and water works." A strong point was also made by the speakers of the fact that the House of Commons was permitting in its own case the very thing which it prohibited in connection with much less important authori- ties—that is, the open advocacy of private inter- ests by public representatives. By the ,time the discussion had reached this stage Mr. BALFOUR seemed to have discovered the mistake he had made. The matter turned out to be one of more importance than he had imagined, and in the end he was glad to accept a friendly suggestion thrown out by Mr. COURTNEY that a committee shcaild be appointed to consider "the very delicate I and almost momentous question in regard to the conduct of members in respect to private bills." bt is n a to be hoped that when this committee has ee ppointed it will be able to provide a sweep- ing remedy for an undoubted evil.

THE DUKE of DEVONSHIRE must Tue G wern- inent's Policy, be now extremely disappointed by the performances of the Govern_ ment of which he is a conspicuous member. In the opinion of the DUKE the one thing which the untry needed above all else and the one thin

===== 0002601_18960418_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EX AMINER SATUROA Y. APRIL 18. 1896. RTJNCORN AND wiDNES.

which the new Government would give it was repose." But, as a matter of fact, there has scarcely ever been a less reposeful period than since this Government came into power. One sensation has followed another, and at the present time there is no promise of repose either in,foreign or domestic affairs. In the sphere of domestic legislation the Education Bill alone will provide numberless bones of contention. The opposition to this measure is strengthening in various direc- tions, and the lengths to which a struggle upon educational subjects may be carried has been strikingly exemplified in Canada. The legislature of that colony sat continuously during the whole of last week in order to make progress with a Bill to settle an education difficulty in Manitoba but no progress was made. Another attempt was made at the beginning of this week to get the Bill through but it proved futile and:the measure had to be abandoned. It is, of course, not at all likely that opposition will be carried to any such extremes as this in the House of Commons, but a Government which brought in the Education Bill can certainly not claim that it has studied the repose of the country. In foreign and colonial matters there is little if any abatement of the " storm and stress "of the last few months, The worst anticipations are being realised with regard to Matabele rising. The insurgent natives appear to increase in numbers daily, and the white colonists are cooped up in thei r stockades or laagers. It now seems as though a considerable force of troops will be :required to suppress this native rising, and in the meantime the colonisa- tion of Rhodesia is effectually stopped. In the North of Africa the latest enterprise of our Tory Government is slowly developing. The invasion and reconquest of the Soudan has now been fairly entered upon, and what is to be the end of that altogether unnecessary adventure no man can foretell.

There is just a probability that the Loudon and North-Western Railway Company may get a Roland for their Oliver in opposing the Warrington Borough Extension Bill. The Parliamentary Committee of the Corporation have deckled to strenuously oppose a Bill promoted by the London and North-Western Railway Company, in which powers are sought to construct a branch line near Warrington. We are not surprised to hear of this decision considering the opposition that the Company is offering to the Extension Bill.

The Salvation Army in Warrington is making considerable progress, and the number of adherents now exceeds two hundred. They are, however,con- siderably handicapped by the want of a suitable meeting place, and the members have now set their hearts upon getting a new " barracks," the present being altogether unsuitable. Already a sum of over £lOO has been placed in the hands of Mr. Ziba Armitage, who has consented to be treasurer, with this object in view, and W 3 wish the army every success in raising the necessary funds.

There was an unusual variety of business at the last Town Council meeting; in fact almost ' every branch of the Corporation work was more or less discussed. This was no doubt largely due to the fact that the annual estimates of expenditure were submitted by the spending committees—and all the committees do something in the way of spending. This occasion is generally taken ad- vantage of by the Chairmen of the Committees to enlarge upon the work of the past year and to fore- cast the operations of the year to come. gouncil- lore Monks and Shaw-Green, in particular, set a good example to chairmen of committees in giving full and lucid explanations in connection with the Sanitary Works and Finance Committees. On the other hand, Alderman Sutton was criticised by some of the junior members of the Council for his reticence in regard to the work of the Health Committee, and there was un- doubtedly some ground for the criticism. The Health Committee is face to face with some im- portant questions, such as hospital extensions and the dealing with insanitary property, and a general forecast of the way the Committee is likely to deal with these and other subjects would have been of interest both to the Council as a whole and to the ratepayers.

DEATH OF MRS. T. WILKES. Wesleyans in Runcorn will learn with regret of the death ot the wife of the Rev. T.Wilkes which took place on the Bth inst. at Rochdale.

RUNCORN BOATING CLUB.

The third and concluding conversaztone of the season was held under the auspices of the above club in the Drill Hall, on Wednesday evening. ,Dancing was kept going to a capital selection of dance music given by an efficient band under the direction of Mr. Powell, of Widnes. The duties of M.C. were ably carried out by Mr. W. Crosby and Mr. Harry Wright. Professor Miller gave an amusing ventriloquial entertainment.

PRESENTATION TO MR. JOHN SIMPSON,

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The annual meeting of the above Council was held on Thursday evening in the Town Hall, for the purpose of electing chairman and vice-chairman of committees : There were present the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. E. Imison, J.P.. S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., H. J. Grice, G. H. Kirkham, W. Handley, J. W. Liehtburn, G. Hewitt, F. Wiswall, W. Collier,J. Horsefield,T. Dean,M. Sweetman, and D. Bisbrown ; Mr. Lake (law clerk), Mr. Marshall (secretary), and Mr. Wilding (surveyor).

ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN AND VICE-CHAIRMAN._

Mr. Imison- said the first business was to elect alchair- man for the ensuing year, and he proposed that Mr. Lightburn be elected to that position. He con- sidered that Mr. Lightbum's experience on the Board of Commissioners and also on that Council must be of great service to the town.—Mr. Once seconded, and Mr. Taylor and Dr. Gastaldi supported, and the resolution was unanimously carried.—Mr. Light- burn thanked teem very cordially for theconfidence they had placed in him by electing him Cnairman of the Council. For his own part as far as he was able he would endeavour to justify that confidence, and would give what ability and energy he had to the business of the Cuncil. (Applause.) He felt his responsibility greater because he had to follow one who had very worthily filled the chair. He hoped that they would all work together for the benefit of the ratepayers. Mr. Bisbrowne, in proposing a vote of thanks to the retiring chairman,said he had discharged thehonour and dignity of that important office in an upright and hon- ourable way which would commend itself to both the Council and the ratepayers. He moved that the best thanks of the meeting be tendered to Mr. Imison for his services as chairman during the year.—Mr. Taylor seconded, and felt sure that no chairman had occupied the position and given greater satisfaction than Mr. Imison had done.—Mr. Handley. in supportingsfelt sure that the chairman would not accuse him of raising a dis- senting voice when he said that he would have been pleased if Mr. Imison would have allowed his name to be put forward as chairman for aeother year. Mr. Imison thanked the Council for their appreciative expressions, and he might say his year of office had been a remarkably pleasant one.—Mr. Pritchard proposed Mr. Imison as vice-chairman for the coming year, and thought that the proposal would receive the highest ap- proval of every member present.—Mr. lAriswall seconded, and Dr. Gastaldi supported, and the motion was unani- mously carried.—Mr. Imison briefly acknowledged. ELECTION OF COMMITTEES. _ _ _

On the motion of Mr. Taylor. seconded by Mr. Dean, it was proposed that the standing committee should con- sist of the whole of the Council.—Mr. Bisbrowne moved and Mr. Wiswall seconded an amendment that with the exception of the Highway Committee—committees should consist of a member from each ward. The amendment was lost. The Highway and Technical In- struction Committees were also appointed.

TELE COUNCIL AND THE TRADE OT THE TOWN:.

Mr. Jones moved the following resolution: "That a committee be appointed to consider the best means of developing the trade of Runcorn,and the advisability of encouraging and attracting new industries in our town." after the very favourable and encouraging expressions of opinion which were given at the last Council meeting regarding an attempt to be made with a view to developing the trade of the town, he now wished to suggest another step in that direction. At the last meeting the opinion was that the object in view vnuld be best attained by getting together a number of gentlemen of means and business capabilities and who would interest themselves with the matter rather than hold a public meeting of ratepayers. Acting on that view he gave notice to move the motion which was on the agenda paper believing it to be the best course to adopt. It makes the committee effectual and powerful, it would have to be a representative one and if possi• ble to have one or two gentlemen representing every branch of industry which might be developed in the town. Under these circumstances it will be necessary to have a large committee, and he had taken it upon himself to name a committee, but if any of the members detected any omission he would be glad if they would mention the name. In addition to his resolution he moved that the committee consist of the members of the Council, and the following gentlemen Messrs. R. Abel, J. Brundret, S. C. Beckett, H. Branch, J. Ball, W. H. Chadwick, J. Comes, W. Fisher, J. R. Frith, Joseph Handley, Thomas Jones, B. Kniveton, T. Kirkham, J. Meadowscroft. F. J. Norman, A. R. Norman, W. Occles- ten, J. Oecleston, R. Pierpoint, R. H. Posnett, J. T. W. Posnett, J. T. W. Potter. S. Pope, W. Rowlands,J. Simp- son, G. Summerfield, P. Stubbs, J. J. Speakman, T. D. Timmins, %V. Timmins, C. Timmins, William Toft, E. Turley, William Upton, T. Ward, Sir J.T.Brunner,M.P., Major Ha z!ehurst, and Dr.Steele.—Dr.Gastaldi seconded. —Mr. Handley, in supporting, thought that for the past few months they had been parading their poverty too much and was of opinion that it was time they stopped it. A tradesman would never draw trade to his shop if he exhibited the notice "Come. I am starving," but would attract trade by showing his goods to the hest ad- vantage, and he did not think they would draw trade to Runcorn by the decrying policy pursued during the past 15 months. Mr. Handley then compared Runcorn with other tewns which had been in similar positions and cited several cases where such towns had recovered their pros- perity by the introduction of other trades in the town.— Mr. Wiswall also supported and said, that they all had felt the necessity of securing outside advice.and,it would be an advantage to them to get gentlemen on the com- mittee who were experts in different matters.—The Clerk pointed out that though there was nothing to prevent the Council appointing the committe.., they had no power as a Couecil to do so, or to spend any of the rate- payers money on the matter.—The resolution was then put and carried, and the committee suggested by Mr. Jones was also approved. The Council then went into committee.

WA.RRINGTON BANKRUPTCY COURT.

FRlDAY.—Ber.ore Mr. Registrar Ridgway. A FRODSHAIa PUBLICAN'S BANIERUPTCY.—Mr. Samuel • Clarke, late of the Cholmondeley Arms, Frodsham, came up for his re-examination in bankruptcy. In answer to questions by Mr. Linaker, solicitor, witness said no turniture was removed from the house before the sale day. Ile denied that he had said he had made every- thing right with all the creditors except Mr. Youd. It was not true that he had filed his petition in order to spite Mr. Youd, or that he had found £l2O or £l3O for his son to go to Dunham Elill. He never got commission from Mr. Kenyon on potatoes bought within ten miles from Frodsha.m. His wife re- ceived a cheque from Mr. Burton, solicitor, last month. It was her money. Be admitted that he sold a lot of beer during the construction or the Ship Canal for Walker's on Fr odsham Marsh. He got a good profit on it but it cost a good deal to take it there. At the time of the sate of his goods he did not make any remarks to the detriment of the sale. It was the second time he had called his creditor?, together. He accounted for his in- solvency by bad trade. The sale at the Cholmondeley Arms did not average a barrel a week Curing the last eighteen months.—Mr. Linaker applied for a further adjournment.—Mr. Potter said so far as he was concerned the examination was closed.—The Regis- trar said he did not see any reason for keeping it open after that day.—Mr. Linaker said it was very unfortun- ate that they could not get hold of the full information. Be asked for a further adjourn-uent.—The Registrar said the only grounds on which he would grant an ad- journment would be on the debtor paying the costs.— Mr. Linaker then consented to the examination being closed. PENKETH.

CONCERT.—On Tuesday night a, concert was given by the St. Mary's Minstrels in the Sankey Sunday school. Dr. Murray was in the chair. The programme consisted of songs, j9kes,and a comic dialogue, the principal per- formers bAng, Messrs. Dakin,Hatton, Plumpton, Clarke, Suttor, Pownall, Walker, and Goulden. The misntrels, have under the tuition of Mr. J. Thompson, improved wonderfully since their last concert, and the audience were highly pleased with their performance. The weather was, of course, against a crowded attendance, but the room was comfortably filled. Miss E. Jeans accompanied the singers en the pianoforte. The proceeds are to be devoted to the camping expenses of the St. Mary's Com- pany, C.L.B.

RUNCORN.

INTERESTING REUNION.

An interesting reunion of old Runcorn Wesleyan Day School scholars took place on Monday night in the Brunswick schoolroom. There was a good attendance, over which Mr. S. Taylor, J.P.,C.C., presided. Amongst others present were—Revs. T. Brackenbury and W. George, Messrs. F. J. Norman, J.P., T. Simister, S.Pope, E. Marshall, D. Bisbrowne, J. D. Jones, B. Crosby, E. Taylor, J. F. Moulton, J. Povah, W. Ra.wlinson, G. Mill- ington (secretary), T. Leech, C. W. Hutchinson (New- castle-on-Tyne),J. Unsworth, W. Lewis, H. W. Jukes,R. Norman, S. Bowers, R. W. Rushton, W. Peacock, George Burrows, W. Kirkham, A. Anderson, R. Webster, W. Millington and others. The Chairman, who was warmly received, said it was with very much delight that he heard a number of friends were organized with a view to making some presentation to Mr. Simpson for his long connection with the Wesleyan Day Schools. He believed he occupied that chair as one of the old scholars of the Wesleyan schools—(applause)—and he did not think there were many older scholars there that night. It must be 46 or 47 years ago since he attended Granville-street schools and at that time Mr. Melville was the master. He remembered Mr. Simpson very well and his almost daily visits to the school during the time he (the speaker) attended. It was always a great pleasure to every boy in the school to see Mr. Simpson'a face. He had always a kind word and a genial way, and he was sure Mr. Simpson's manner was a great encouragement to the boy., in helping them en with their tasks. Mr. Simpson had not only taken an interest in the Wesleyan day schools for they all knew that be had been a great educa- tionalist for many years. (Applause.) When the School Board was first formed they knew that Mr. Simpson was one of the first members appointed on the Board, and that for many years he took a most active part in the work of the Board and gave his valuable experience. Be had devoted the whole of his life to education and Sunday school work, and it must have been his great leve for children which had made it possible for him to carry on the work for half a century and to devote a great part of his time to the school, and to sacrifice his Sunday for so many years. He hoped they would have MrSinopson with them for many years to give them the benefit of his valuable experience. (Applause.) He would call on Mr.Millingtoa to make the presentation. Mr. Millington,in making the presentation,said he had been asked totake part in the meeting as a representa- tive old scholar of the Wesleyan Day Schools. Be went to the infant school about the year 1854 when Miss Morris was the mistress and from that school he passed into what was known as the "Top school." At that time the school was a mixed one of boys and girls arid that arrangement was continued until the year 1562. After passing through the school as a scholar he was ap- pointed pupil teacher and was engaged as such for a considerable time. They would see therefore that he had had a long connection with the schools and since then he had taken a great interest in the work of the teachers and the progress of the school. Although he had had a long connection with the school it was but short when compared to that of Mr. Simpson, for be had been the correspondent or he might say the manag,erof the schools from the year 1848 down to last year when the schools were taken over by the School Board. That would mean a connection of about 48 years,and that without any fee or reward save the satisfaction he must have felt in being able to assist the cause of elementary education. He was not able tafgivel the number of scholars that had passed through the schools but he would roughly estimate it at 13,000 children. Some people might say that the woik had been Mr. Simp- son's hobby, and he had done the work as a pleasure. Though he granted that it was so, it did not lessen the good work that had been done, at least, he for one thought not. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Simpson had given time, talent and money in the past towards hel pine' for- ward elementary education in the Wesleyan Schools at Runcorn, and as one of the representative scholars he had pleasure in testifying to the work he had done, and in presenting him on behalf of the old scholars with the casket and the address, showing how thoroughly the ser- vices had been appreciated. The address read by Mr. Millington was as follows "To John Simpson Esq., of Dunsdale, Frodsham." Dear Sir, We, the undersigned, on behalf of the old scholars of the Runcorn Wesleyan Day Schools, desire to recognize your services to Elementary Education in Runcorn, now that the Wesleyan schools have been ac- quired by the Runcorn School Board. Ever since their institution in 1848, you have performed the important duties, of Secretary and correspondent for the schools with characteristic zeal, and efficiency. In that capacity both teachers and scholars found in you a sincere friend, many of whom date their first step towards success in life, to the kindly interest you took in their welfare. it. great success of Elementary education in the town was largely due to the ungrudging labours which you have bestowed upon the schools, and it must be a source of satisfaction to you, as it is a cause of gratification to us to look back on such a long and useful career. We there- fore, beg you to accept this token of the affectionateire- gard in which you are held, by those who have partici- pated in the advantages which your inestimable ser- vices have done so much to provide. That the continued smiles and blessing of Almighty God may rest upon you is the fervent prayer of yours sincerely W. Holland, E. Hutchinson, E. Taylor, J. Wilson, J. Unsworth, T. Chad- wick, D. Sirnkin, J. D. Jones, D. Bisbrown F. J. Nor- man, H. Jukes, B. Crosby, J. F. Moulton, WAlillington, M. J. Lightfoot, M. E. Norman, Carrie Litter, Greenway Millington, (secretary). Mr. Simpson in acknowledging the gift said it was a difficult thing to know what to say on such an occasion as that, as it only happened once in a life-time and hav- ing had no experience in such occasions before it was difficult to know how best to acknowledge the great kindness they had shown him in making that (presenta- tion. He did feel himself sincerely thankful to them for their kindness and it was certainly a proof of the feelings of his friends towards him. (Applause.) Mr. Simpson then referred at some length to the transference of the Wesleyan schools to the School Board.He said that at the time it was said that it was a very serious thing indeed for Runcorn to have the expenses of carrying on the school thrust upon the ratepayers, but the last meeting of the School Board showed that tie precept for the half year was only half the amount that it was last year and the reason was because the Wesleyan schools had handed over an amount of between £2OO and £3OO, which came to them from the Government Grant and placed the School Board in quite a different position. Mr. Simpson, in concluding, again thanked those present for their kindness to him and for their handsome presentation.

Mr. J.D. Jones, after giving reminisences of his schocl days, said he thought that it was a blessing that the schoolmaster was abrmad. His work was at the founda- tion of the national life. Yet there was a sneaking feel- ing that they were doing too much in that direction. In heavily-taxed America they did not grumble at their education rate which they in England would ca:l exces- sive,because the Americans knew that they were getting their money's worth, and he thought it would be a black day for England when the word was given to retrench in the matter of education. Scotland had had the ad- vantage of them in competition for places where know- ledge was required, simply through the splendid system of education which for many generations had been the birthright of every boy. What education had done for Scotland it would do for England. The function of the schoolmaster was to give elastic minds their proper bent, and he thought there shoutd be no barrier be- tween the school desks and the national seats of learn- ing, at Oxford and Cambridge except those which could be overcome by persistent energy on the part of the stu- dent. The retrogade measure which was now before Par- liament would never be allowed to place hindrance in the way of the rising generation. (Hear,hear,and applause.) They honoured such men as Mr. Simpson who in the dark days held aloft the lamp of knowledge, and lured the people with whom he had the most influence to the paths of progress and only gave up when the State fully awakened to its duty and took over the education of its youth. Such a man deserved the highest reward that an enlightened community could bestow upon him. (Applause.) He hoped the spirit ef such men as Mr. Simpson would always be found in the good old town. (Hear, hear.) Mr. W. Millington said in representing the younger generation of old scholars he could simply join in the congratulations given to Mr. Simpson that night on the occasion of the completion of 50 years of work, in con- nection with education in Runcorn. Mr. Samuel Pope and others also briefly addressed the meeting, which closed with the usual votes of thanks.

ALLEGED WIFE DESERTION.—Patrick Burke, described as an ironworker last Irons Wigan, was brought up ou a warrant charged with having on the sth August, 1891, neglected to maintain his wife and two children, who had becawe chan,e- able to the common fund of the Warrington Union. Mr. Basil Shearer appeared on behalf of the Board of Guardians, and stated that the prisoner's wife and family became charge- able on the 6th August, 1891, and remained in the workhouse until the 28th of that month. They left and were re-admitted In 1892 for a hort time. Since 1892 the prisoner had sup- ported his wife and children, and they had been living to gether at Wigan. They were willing to pay the amount owing to the Guardians, 485., if sufficient time was allowed them.— Prisoner expressed his willingness to pay 6s. a wee {.-31r, Shearer That will satisfy me. —The case was adjourned for- eight weeks, and the prisoner was told that he would have to pay the 485. in the meantime.

PRESENTLTION TO A FORMER RUNCORNIAN. From the Adelaide (South Australian) " Register " we gather that Mr. Kirkham Evans, who some years ago carried on business as a draper in High-street, Runcorn, has been the recipient of a prest3nt from his workpeople. The occasion was the severance of Mr. Evans' connection from the firm of Messrs. Kirkham Evans and Co.,tailors, Gawler-street, Adelaide. The present—a handsome dressing case—was bought by subscriptions from the employees of the firm, amongst whom general regret was expressed that Mr. Evans was giving up business.

During Sunday a rumour was set aft,at that the body of Captain Hough had been found. rhough this turned out to be a mistake there was some ground for the rumour,as a body was found at G.rston, but the des- cription did not tally with that of the missing Runcorn captain. EXCITING SCKNE AT TOP LOCKS, RUNCORN. On Tuesday night a seaman belonging to the schooner "Emma Grace," h d an exciting and unpleasant adven- ture in the vicinity of Top Locks, Runcorn. He was walking along the Bridgewater canal balik when by some unaccountrble means he slipped and tell into the canal. Rekg a swimmer he had no difficulty in keep- ing himselt afloat. He was assisted on to the canal bank little the worse for his immersion. RUNCORN CYCLING CLUB.

About twenty members of the above club assembled at headquarters on Wednesday afternoon and proceeded to Newton Lakes. From Warrington the outing became an inter-club run with the Northwich club. On arriv- ing at Newton a good substantial tea was proc ided by Mrs. Gorst, of the Oak 'free Hotel, and having done ample justice to it, the cyclists pleasantly passed their time on the lake and bowling greens. The return jour- ney was commenced about 6 30. This was the first official run of the Widnes lay section of the team. THE "DUSKY DIAMOND 3 "AT RUNCORN in our advertisement columns is announced a per- formance in aid of the Runcorn Branch of the Mersey Mission to Seamen. The performance under the name of' Dusky Diamonds," will be given by the inemb2rs of the Kentucky Darkies Amateur Minstrel Society, of Liverpool, and in the hands of this well-known troupe the" show "is certain to be exceptiodally good. We understand the Kentuckians have raised over £4,000 for various charities since they first began to black their faces—a record they may well be proud of. Our readers should not miss this performance or the opportunity it affords of supporting a very deserving charity. THEATRE ROYAL, RUNCORN. At this theatre a capital play entitled " The Zingara " has been staged, and the company headed by Mr. H. R. Grindall is one of all round excellence. Mr. Grindall appears in his original part" Carlos " a eipsy chief, and Miss C. Dunbeer makes a powerful " Zonetta." Mr. Frank Weston is also well cast as Norman Temple, and Miss Clarice Mignon gives a good representation of the gipsy boy. The comic element, lies in the hands of Messrs. Graham and Walton and is ably sustained. Amongst specialities introduced may be mentioned the Sisters Leonard, clever song and dance artistes who have an up to date songlon the new woman, Miss Dollie and Mr. Will Conquest, smart speciality dancers, Miss Clarice Mignon (instrumentalist), and Messrs. F. Weston and J. S. Graham. VOLUNTARY V. BOARD SCHOOLS.

An inter society debate was held on Wednesday even- ing at the meeting of the Brunswick Improvement Class, Runcorn, between the members of the St. Paul's Institute class and the members of the home class. The chair was occupied by Mr. T. Simister, and there was a large attendance. The subject of the debate was "Which is best for education,voluntary or Board schools," and it was opened by the Rev. W. Stanton Jones on behalf of voluntary schools. The speaker mainly alluded to the religious education given therein. Mr. J. D. Jones.who followed, took the opposite view, and pointed out the advantages possessed by Biard schools from an educa- tional standpoint. The opener was supported by a visitor, Mr. Lunt, who was afterwards succeeded by Mr. J. Moulton. The debate was then thrown open, and Messrs. B. Crosby, W. Millington, and D. J. Brookes (Widnes),participated. The Rev..T.S. Jones was allowed the right of reply. Votes of thanks were accorded to the Widnes gentlemen for coming to Runcorn and fur- nishing the class the opportunity of spending such an interesting and profitable evening. TILEFTS FROM HIGIIFIELD TANNERY. RUNCORN.

On Tuesday afternoon Thomas James McQuinn, aged 23 years, of 44, Egerton-street, was charged at the Run- corn Police Court, before Messrs. Norman and Imison, with stealing two skins, value 65., the property of the Highfield Tannery Co.. between the 2nd and 13th inst. Mr. R.H.Posnett,manager of the works,said accused had been in the employment of the firm for about three or four months as a labourer. They had missei portions of two skins.—A witness named Dunbobbin spoke to missing McQuinn and his dinner basket on the previous day, and suspicions having been aroused the basket was examined and the skin found in it.—P.C. Gregory stated that he apprehended the prisoner at his father's house in Fox-street about noon that day and took him to the police station where he denied having taken the first skin but owned to taking the second. He fo.und a quantity of rough bits of hide.—lt was stated that defendant had been experimenting in the work of leather tanning with odds and ends, he having an idea that he had discovered a more expeaitious way of doing the work. The magistrates bound defendant over on his own rezognisances to come up for judgment when called on.

MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL BILL.

(Continued from page 3.)

On Thu rsday Mr. Blake's Committee of the House of Commons continued the consideration of the Omnibus Bill of the Manchester Ship Canal Company, the part under consideration being that proposing to close the present tld9l rele‘hgs. Mr. W. H. Hunter, engineer of the Canal Company, was recalled for further cross-examination. Replying to Mr. Lloyd, representing the Cheshire County Council, he said he could not admit that the daily discharge from Latchford locks was not sometimes much greater than three million cubic feet. There was practically no dis- charge from the Walton locks. Mr. Pembroke Stephens said he desired to state to the committee the exact position of the borough of Warring- ton this part of the Bill. Under the existing works of the Canal Company there had been a certain diminution of the water level which formerly existed at Warrington. Warrington was advised that what was now proposed would do something to diminish the mischief they had sufferediand therefore to that extent Warrington viewed with favour the proposals of the Bill,as they would bene- fit the borough,aud desired that they should pass. This was,of course, a ,arge question,affecting many interests, and therefore Warrington preserved the right to watch the preceedings,and in the event of any alteration in the present proposals by which Warrington's interests might be affected their attitude would be changed. Mr. Pope, Q.C. (leading for the Mersey Dock Board), said it was most irregular for this statement to be made at this stage. . 1 Mr. Pope afterwards proceeded to ask questions about the proposed working of the sluices and the difference of time in high water at Runcorn and at Waltop, and in I reply, Mr. Hunter said that on a 19ft. 61n. tide high 1 water at Walton would be 2h. 10min. later than at Run- corn, and at that time the Walton sluices would be fully open. The Old Randle,s and Weaver sluices would be gradually opened, so that they would enable them to discharge the full ebb down to tho 14ft. 12m. level. The question of the discharge from the Walton sluices would be materially affected as to its efficiency by the period when that discharge would reach the incoming flood,and he calculated that the discharge would be completed nine hours before the termination of the ebb.

Slr Henry ROECJO, scientific adviser to the Joint Com- mittee for the Mersey and Irwell watershed, produced .amples of the Ship Canal water taken at different places below the Latchford locks. The result of his analyses was to show that the composition of the water below that point was really unexceptionable. There was no appreciable amount of sewage c)ming down. Be was sorry to say this was not the case with the higher por- tions of the canal.

Mr. W. H. Whec-Aer, civil engineer, who bad had ex- perience for thirty wears of tidal rivers, gave evidence, corroborating Mr. Hunter, to the effect that the closing of the tidal cpenings would be an advaiatage to the navi- gation of the canal, and would reduce the amount of dredging now required. At the same time the estuary would not be injured. Mr. Pope observed that in 1885 Mr. Wheeler gave evi- dence in favour of the tidal openings. The Chairman: I hope we are all wiser as we grow older. Mr. Pope: It should modify the confidence with which we express our opinions._

Mr. Vernon Hai:court, civil engineer, knd Sir B. Baker, President of the Institute of Civil Engineers, gave cor- raborative testimony, and as the learned counsel for the petitioners did not show much Inclination to cross- examine, Mr. Moulton said he would not trouble the Committee with any further engineering evidence. He would be Prep Ared to enter into the navigation part of the case for the promoters next day. The Committee then adjourned.

COUNTY OF CHESTER. SWINE FEVER.

NOTICE is Hereby Given, that the Board of AOcul- tore have, by an ORDER dated the 14th day of Anril, 1896, REVQI:CED, as from the 21st day of Aptil, 1896, their Order dated the 7th day of December, 1894, declaring-, amongst others, an Area comprising the County of Chester and the Boroughs of Birkenhead, Chester, Congleton, Crewe, Hyde, Macclesfield, Staly- bridge and S.tockport,to be an Area infected with Swine Fever for the purpose of the Swine Fever (Infected Areas) Order of 1894, No. 3. The effect of the foregoing Revocation is that. Swine (whether Fat or Store) may, from and after the 21st day ot April, 1896, be moved out of the County of Chester without License. Dated this 15th day of April, 1896, . REGINALD POTTS, • Clerk to the Cheshire County Coun6il. Chester.

IrTF EXAMINER, SATURDAY. APRIL 18, 1896.

NEWTON PETTY SESSIONS,

34TunDIr.TBe1ore Mr. T. J. Gillespie (chairman), Colonel

Wood. Messrs. R. Stone, and b. B. F. Borron.

THE SELLING OF " LUCKY PACKETS "—Thorns--Stokes. was charged with having on the 2nd inst., been a dealer in '* lucky packets,"—P.S. Bancks stated that •at ten minutes pest eight on the 2nd mat, the defendant was in charge of a stall at Barlestown market. He had a basket full of small packets which he sold at a penny each. Some of the packets contained tickets only, and others entitled the purchaser to a prize. Witness in company with P.C. Turner, purchased a packet and obtainerl a purse as a prize '1 he Chairman said it was the first case they bad had before them of that kind and a nominal fine of is. and costs would be imposed.— Thomas Melbille was charged with 'a similar offence on the 2nd inst. Defendant did not appear.—P.S. Bancks gave evidence as to purchasing a packet from the defendant which entitled him to a pocket book P.C. Turner purchased one, and he re- ceived a ring.—The Chairman : You don't seem to have done so badly in your purchases.—P.S. Barnes Lots of children sp.tat shillings'withthe defendant and got nothing. —A fine of ss. and costs was imposed. AN BARLESTOWN G Froesm's WEre-ters.—Michael Meliermott, grocer, Derby-street, Berlestown, was charged with having in his posse,sion for use in trade two four lbs. weights, and one two lbs. weight, which were unjust.— Mr. James Shepherd, inspector of weights and measures, stated that on the 3:th March he visited the defendant's shop and found a four lb. weight which was six sirahms light. another one four draehms light, and one two lb. weight, two drachms light—Defendant, who did not appear, was fined 10s. and costs, and the weights were ordered to be forfeited. POACHERS IN TROURLW—SamueI Mains and John Williams were charged with trespassing in pursuit of game on the morning of the 22nd March.—Williams p'eaded guilty, but Mains did not app.er.—Mr. Stuart, solicitor, prosecuted, and stated that a man named Henry Squires saw the defend- ant in company with the other man mentioned, on land in the occupation of the exeentors of the late Mr. Stone, trying some rabbit burrows. On seeing Squires they went away, but were afterwards found on the land in th occupation of Mr. Kr:l.foot at Winwick. This time of the year was the worst period to disturb rabbits, and ny doing so the defend- ants caused more damage than if they took one.—Henry squires. watchman in the employ of Mr. Stone, eonfirmei Mr. Stuart's statement. The defendants were in possession of a ferret and nets,—Williams was fined 109. and costs, and a warrant was issued against M rins. David Ma wson.of Hay- dock, was also charged with having on the 3rd April at Hay- dock,cornrnitted trespass by entering certain land in the ()ICU- pi.tion of John Owen in search of game. Defendant. admitted being on the land but not in ti.,arrh of rabbits. -Mr. Stuart, solicitor, said he prosecuted on behalf of Lord Newton. On the evening of the 30th ult , a witness named Wood. nephew of Mr. Owen's, was going over his uncle's farm, and he saw a man he knew to be a companion of the defendant's. He came to the conclusion that Mawson was not far off. On going a little further he saw Mawson standing near a rabbit hole, and afterwards sew a ferret come from it Mawson put the nets in his polkets and said 14 was searching for rats. What made the offence worse was that in February last, the defendant was fined 20s. and costs for a similar offence, not one hundred yards from the place where he was seen by Wood —Alfred John Wood gave corroborative evidence. The defendant was accompanied by a dog. He told witness that he had been ferreting for rats, but witness told him there were n rats in a rabbit hole.—Samuel Watkins, keeper in the employ of Lord Newton, also gave evidence.—A fine of 20s. and costs was imposed. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

ELLESMERE-ST. MISqION.-ANNIVERSARY SERVICES.

The Rev. J. Kirtlan, formerly superintendent of the Warrington circuit, but now of Doncaster, preached the agniversary services in connection with Ellesmere-street Mission on Sunday. Services were conducted there, morning and evening, and were attended by large con- gregations. The Rev. J. Kirtlan expressed t-e pleasure it (7ave-liiin to renew his acquaintance with the people at the Ellesmere-street Mission. He preached two ex- cellent sermons, and in the afternoon conducted a ser- vice in Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel. The subject of his address was " Song, and its place in the worship of God." Special solos were sung by Master Charles Baker, of Manchester, including " Angels ever bright and fair," and "I know that my Redeemer liveth." His singing was greatly appreciated. The coll-ctions taken durinc, the day on I ehalf of Ellesmere-street Mission, realised £8 13s.

The public meeting held on Monday evening in Bold- street Wesleyan schoolroom was presided over by Mr. Henshall, and he was supported on the platform by the Revs. Owen Watkins, J. Gunnell, W. G. Triggs, and J. Kirtlan. After devotional exercises, the Chairman said h. thought the friends at Ellesmere-street Mission made a happy selection when they invited the Rev. J. Kirtlan to come to the anniversary services. He was sure that if there was one place in the Warrington cir- cuit where the people had a warm place in their hearts for Mr. Kirtlan it was at Ellesmere-st. (Ap- plause.) He was sure Mr. Kirtlan would be delighted with the reception he got. on coming amongst them again.He hoped that the offer of Mr.lsaac to give £5O to the mission if another £5O were raised would be taken up. IF they raised thst amount the debt on the mission would he wiped off, and the friends at Ellesmers-st. would be able to commence with a clean sheet.--Miss Rose Blackhurst and Mr. C. P. Price contributed two vocal items in an admirable manner.—Mr. J. E. Barlow gave an interesting account of the work being clime at the mission. He said they had a capital Sunday school with about 250 names on the school books. The Band of Hope was going on successfully, and there was also a penny bank to which the scholars subscribed from a penny upwards. Durinri the year they had paid over 1,60. (Hear, hear.) The bank in- volved considerable labour—Mr. Gregson and himself had to be there every week—but they were trying to en- courage thrift. (Anplause.) They were looking forward to brighter and happier days at the Mission, and they hoped their friends would help thern"liberally. Towards the .-c250 which they honed to raise, they had already in hand £39 183. 61., and several promises of help had been made.—Songs were now agreeably rendered by Miss Hannah Broinsh and Mr. S. Hassan, and Miss Bessie Dutton. of Moore, held the audience in rapt attention while she read a piece from Dickens's works.—The Rev. J. Kirtlan, in the course of a short address, said that whilst there was a great deal of enthusiasm in many places for missions in Manchester and London, to what they called the masses, there did not appear to be the same amount of enthusiasm towards missions which were in their own midst. Whilst anxious for the success of missions elsewhere, he thought charity should begin at home. In Warrington there were probably thousands who never went to a place of worship, and it was very inconsistent for them to be enthusiastic for mission work elsewhere and apathetic ' about such work in their midst. He was glad that there , were some who saw the importance of mission work in , their own town, and who wer t trying to do good work in a neighbourhood that needed it. The speaker then Procesteded to trace the history of the mission which commenced in a humble way in a schoolroom. When the property was sold they were obliged to go elsewhere and the present mission-room in Ellesmere-street was :trected. To make the mission a complete success several things were needed. It was necessary that 'here should he a large and thoroußhly well- 'rained and disciplined choir, It was also the du+y of every member of the Wesleyan church t, see that the mission did not lack workers and funds. He referred to the 250 children attending the Sunday school. He did not think there was enrugh raver in the Stinds7 schools. There were some Sunday ;chools where there was scarcely a prayer offered from quarter's end to another/ If the Sunday schools —re to he a success th.re must be more prayer by ; -ambers an(' scholars. In conclusion, he urged all to be z in C'sri‘tian work.—sons were afterwards sums Miss R >se Illackhurst,Miss Hannah Brough, and Mr. ; price. The meeting terminated with the Passim" of a vote of thanks on the motion of the Rev. W. G. Triggs, seconded by Mr. Levi Richardson.

ST. PAUL'S CHURCH SALE OF WORK.

The annual sale of work held in connection with St. Paul's Church,Warrington, was opened in the Kendrick. street school, by Mrs. Greenall, of Grappenhall There was a numerous attendance at the initial pro- ceedings including the Rev. H. V. and Mrs. Pigot, the Rev. H. Brierlev,Mrs. Brierley,Mrs. Braide, Mr. J. Law- less, Mr., Mrs. and Miss Finney, Mrs. Parr, Mrs. Logan Wright, Mrs. H. Greenall,Mr. C. White, and the Misses White, Mr. and Mrs. W. Chantrv, Mr. J. White,Captain Chorley, Rev. J. C. and Mrs. Pigot (St. Helens), Mrs. Beamont„ Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, Mrs. W. D. Jeans, Alderman Sutton, Dr. Nuttall, Mr. and Mrs. A. Pigot, Mr. and Mrs. Irving, Mr. H. Pigot, Miss Birley, Miss Ainsworth, Miss Comber' Mrs. Alderman Harrison, Mrs- P. Greenall, and Mrs. V. Davies. There were nine stalls in the room and all were well stocked with an infinite variety of articles, from a comfortable wicker chair to a parrott and cage. Wearing ap- parel was of course the main thing for sale, and of this there was an excellent choice. With the bright stalls,the coloured maps and drawings on the walls, and a little fancy work attached to the gas brackets, the schoolroom presented a gay and cheerful appearance,by no means detracted from by the number of people present. The stallholders were as follows : Stall I. Mrs. Gibson Smith, Mrs. Edwin Woods,and Mrs. Peacocke ; (2) Miss Geddes and Mrs. Hepherd ; (3) Mrs. Chantry and Miss Chantry ; (4) Mrs. Finney and Miss Finney ; (5) Miss Atherton, Mrs. F. Atherton and Mrs. J. Atherton ; (6) Miss Lytlagoe, Miss Craik, and Miss Robinson ; (7) refreshment stall, Miss Westwell, MrrG. Braide, Miss Braide, Mrs. Fryer, Miss Sewart (Wigan), and Miss Hulda Houghton ' • (8) flower stall,Mrs.Markell, Miss Melcolm (Belfast), and Miss Bottomley (London) ; (9) Miss Ethel and Miss Bessie Richardson; bran pie, Miss L. Rathbone and Miss E. Pickton. Previous to calling upon Mrs. Greenall, the VICAR. the Rev. H. V. Pigot, said they were met once more on an occasion which for their parish was a very important one, namely to open the annual sale of work. He had explained in former years how very necessary the sale of work was to the success of the parish. On the last occasion he made what seemed to be rather a startling statement to some, that St. Paul's parish in Warrington was the poorest and started £lOO a year behind the next poorest. That fact made it necessary that they should adopt all legitimate means of raising money in order that the work that was going on there could be carried on without interruption. He would give them a tew figures to show what the sale had done during the last 5 years. The first year the amount distributed amongst the various organisations of the parish was £lO6 74. Id., which was followed annually with £140.£160, £l6l,and £l7O for last year. (Hear, hear.) The first charge upon fhe funds, was always Miss Westwell's salary, though there were other claims to be satisfied. The curate fund looked for large help from the sale, and the £5O it had last year was only Just enough to make what they re- quired, with the subscriptions and the diminished grant which the Curates' Aid Society now gave them. The rev, gentleman concluded by expressing regret at the absence through ill-health of Mr. Robert Davies. He was the senior patron of the Church, and they always liked him with them on this occasion. Mr. GREENALL said she was extremely grateful to her nephew, their Vicar, for allowing her the opportunity of appearing before them on that occasion. She hoped that the weak manner in which she opened the sale would not have an injurious effect upon it, but that all present would do their best to raise money to enable alr. Pigot to meet the numerous claims of the parish. It afforded her much pleasure in declaring the sale open. (Applause.) Mr. D. W. FINNEY proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs. Greenall in the absence of the peo?le's warden. Mr. CHANTRY, as vicar's warden, seconded. The motion was heartily carried, and the sale of work was then proceeded with. The total realised was £153 39, 6d.
