WARRINGTON v. WAKEFIELD TRINITY.

The ban of suipension over the Wakefield; Trinity ground was the cause of the well-known Trinitarians visiting the Wilderspool Enclosure= Saturday afternoon for the second tme this season. There was an average attendance Wakefield travelled with a representative team, but Warrington were short of several of their usual players. Tbe teams were :

WARRINGTON :/—Full back, J. W. Evans; three quer, tem. Broady, Burton, O'Brien, Isherwood; half-backs- Higginbottom and Clarke; forwards, Hill, Nevins,Taylor Fairhurst, Saunders, Berry, Burrows, and Thornton. WAKRFIELD TRINITY : Full back, Todd; threequarte rs, Price, Lockwood,Elowell, Foster; half-backs, Brakewell , and Mils m ; forwards, E4oe, Thresh, Walton, Alchurch , Woodhead, Dawson, Price, and Moorheuse. The Wakefield captain winning the toss elected to play with the advantage of the wind during the first half, and Nevens -commenced operations, kicking to- wards the pavilion goal. A passing movement initiated by the home forwards, and taken up by Burton, O'Brien and Isherwood, carried play- to the visitors' 25. Lock- wood was next prominent with a good run, but the effort was somewhat polled by a pass forward. Broady received a nasty kick on the ankle, and had to retire for a short time. His place was taken by Nevins, and the forwards made up this deficiency by combined play. The visitors were kept busy defending their lines some time, tvut relief came to them at length by taking advantage of the erractic passing of the Warrington half backs,arad play was transferred to Warrington territory. Higgin- bottom made a useful run, and Fairhurst grassed Brakevrell when in the act of kicking out. Howell made amends shortly afterwards for with a flying kick he transferred plsy to midfield. A kick of Heesom's was smartly returned and Isherwood daringly saved. A strong run by Burton who afterwards passed to Broady was next witnessed. BroAdy,despite his injury,ran well into the visitors' twenty-five. A penalty kick was awarded the homesters in a good position. Nevins took the kick against the stiff breeze and !ailed to open the scoring. Bringing the ball with them inta the home quarters, the Wakefield team were allowed a penalty kick owing to Heesom not playing the ball immediately. Howell took the kick and landed a fine goal from near the halfway line. Soon after the 1 kick-out, Lockwood intercepted a pass and romped splendidly down the field. O'Brien was, however, too smart for him, and floored him when near the line. The homesters now played up with 'vigour, and a passing movement taken part in by Fligginbottom,Burton, Isher- wood, and Broady almost broke through the Wakefield def rice, Howell proving the stumbling block. Warring- ton were near the line when the whistle blew for half- time, the score standing, Wakefield one goal (three points), Warrington nil. Thresh restarted for the visi- tors, and Fairhurst returned. Give-and-take play en- sued for sometime, but Warrington eventually assumed the aggressive, and the visitors' defence was severely tested. Penalty kicks were taken by Burton and Hig- ginbottom without anything tangible being scored. By determined forward rushes, Wakefield were enabled to carry play into the home quarters, but Burton responded with a long punt, three parts the length of the field, and Todd was unsuccessful in getting in his retorn kick. A scrimmage ensued near the line, and Clarke getting possession crossed the line amidst cheers. Nevins took the kick and placed a beautiful goal. Shortly afterwards Saunders almost scored,owing to the fumbling of the visiting backs, Todd cleverly saving. A number of uninteresting scrimmages and line-outs fol- lowed in which the visiting forwards seemed to have an advantage. At length O'Brien took a pass and after a smart run he parted with the ball to Burton who raced over the line and placed the ball behind the posts. Nevins again took the kick and was once more success- ful. The game soon afterwards concluded, Warrington winning by two goals (ten points), to Wakefield Trinity one penalty goal (three points). OLDHAM T. RUNCORN.

The interest taken in —tins— dx-Lure is amply provided I by the fact that something like 8,000 spectators as- sembled on the Oldham ground to witness the match played off, and a goodly proportion of this number would be made up by visitors from Runcorn, who took the opportunity afforded by the Railway Company of visiting Oldham at an exceptionally cheap rate. The game itself was commenced in good form, and Oldham for some time, in spite of the wind, held the advantage. The first break away on the part of the visitors was by M. Cutcheon and Martin who got over the Runcorn quarter line, but passing by Faulkner, Butterworth and West,relieved the visitors' lines. Whylie and Childs with a dashing dribble, gave Oldham the advantage again, and Varley made a mark, from which England essayed a goal but the wind carried the shot out of all danger. Myers, however, showing considerable speed, kicked the ball into touch, and from this play proceeded almost at fever heat. The Runcorn backs by virtue of their steady defensive play proved a hard nut to crack, but a passing display all along the line of the Oldham backs gave Hurst a long sought opportunity and passing out of Floughton's reach he managed to score in the corner. The:Oldhareites 'continued the attack with varying suc- cess, the forwards playing with commendable dash, but up to half time the game remained unaltered. In the seclnd half, Oldham made free use of the wind, and attacked in a vigorous manner the defence of the visi- tors. After one of tbe Runcorn backs had his kick charged, Childs finished a try behind the posts, and Lees settled the game, practically by placing the goal. Runcorn, beaten but playing well, were again deteated, by bril- liant passing by which Hirst again got over, and shortly afterwards Martin made a fine effort and kicked over the line,and Arthur Lees following up completed the try. This made the result figure out at a goal and three tries (14 points), for Oldham to Runcorn nothing.

PAD GATE.

PARISH COUNCIL MEETING.

A meeting of this Parish Council was held in the Schoolroom, Padgate, on Wed nesday evening. All the members were present, namely, Messrs. Mason, Thorn- ton, Royston, Mather, Roberts, W. Houghton, and Granger (clerk). The first business was the election of chairman for the coming year. Mr. Mather proposed that Mr. Royston be re-elected. Mr. Royston had, he said, served the town- ship faithfully and well for many years.—Mr. Houghton seconded this proposition which was carried without dissent, though Messrs. Thornton and Mason did not vote.

• The appointment of overseers being the next business, Mr. Thornton proposed that Messrs. John Houghton, J. Mason, J. Critchley, and J. Roberts be appointed for the coming year. Mr. Granger seconded the proposition.— Mr. Houghton: What is the use of having four ?—Mr. Thornton : In the multitude of counsellors there is wis- dom. In any case we can do no harm.—The Clerk said the Council was entitled to elect four, two of whom would take the place of the churchwardens who were overseers by virtue of their office.—Mr. Houghton failed to see where the old overseers had been remiss in the discharge of their duties, and he therefore proposed the re-appointment of Messrs. J. Houghton, T. Bennett, and G. Mather.—This proposal was, however, not seconded, and Mr. Thorntph's motion was carried. Mr. Thornton was then, on the motion of Mr. R:berts seconded by Mr. Mason, elected vice-chairman of the Council.

With regard to the waywardenship, Mr. Houghton proposed that Mr. Royston be again re-appointed, and Mr. Mather seconded.—Mr. Boberts proposed the ap- pointment of• Mr. Thornton and Mr. Mason seconded.— The Clerk supported the nomination of Mr. Thornton, as he believed there was a desire of a change in the village.—Mr. Thornton was, therefore appointed. Mr. B.evers, of the National Provincial Bank, was ap- pointed treasurer to the Council and Mr. Granger was re-appointed clerk. A finance committee consisting of the chairman, and Messrs. Thornton,Mason and Mather was also appointed. The Clerk said he had received a letter from Mr. J. L. Harrington, enclosing a memorial signed by several local residents asking the Council to call a parish meeting at an early date to consider the proposal of the Warringtim Corporation to erect a smallpox hospital in the neigh- bourhood. Some discussion took place upon this subject and there was a unanimous opinion that if it was the intention of the Corporation to convey the patients through Padgate and Fearnhead a meeting should be called and a protest made. It was ultimately agreed that the Clerk should write to the Town Clerk to defin- itely ascertain if it was intended to bring the patients through the township. If the answer was in the affirm- ative steps were to be taken to call the meeting of inhabitants.

A general discussion next took place with reference to the condition of some of the footpaths in the township, but as it: was explained that the Council had no authority in this matter no resolution was passed.

WYCLIFFE CONCIIIEGA.TIONAL CHURCH.

SERMON ON "THE NEW WOMAN."

There was an excellent attendance at Wycliffe Con- gregational Church, Warrington, on Sunday evening, when the pastor, the Rev John Yonge, preached a special sermon to young. women on" The New Wonaan." As a text applicable to the subject the rev. gentleman selected "Behold, I make all things new," Rev. 21c, 5v.,' and." If any woman be in Christ she is a new creature,' Cor. 2c., 17v. He said, in commencing that there could be no qusetion that this age, in which we live, was a very remarkable and wonderful ace; they saw manifold and great developments on every hand. Things to-day were very far ahead of what they were 50 years ago. In no century that the world had yet known had there been such rapid onward strides and such marvellous changes as in the century how nearing its end; in fact, it was no exaggeration to say that, in comparison with ail the ages of the past, this was more and more distinctl y the new age, in which old things were passing away and all things, under the control and government of. God, were becoming new. Among the developments of this mar- vellous age there was, Perhaps, none that was more re- markable than the advance made by woman towards her rightful place of equality with man. That, be it understood, was a direct evidence of the influsince of Christianity ; and woman owed her social as truly as her personal redemptioh to Jesus Christ. There could hardly be any feature of the present day life that was better, brighter, more hopeful than the unmistakable advance to wider life and liberty that was being made by woman. They could, of course, have no sympathy whatever with any kind or line of life that could tend to unsex woman, to harden or coarsen her nature, to rob her of her essential domesticity, to destroy the charm of her sweet and tender sensitiveness. They all felt that the purity, comfort, and peace of home life, the sweet and tender sanctities of family affection, supremely depended upon the queenly yet sisterly in- fluence of unselfish womanhood; still the day had gone by in which woman could be shut up in the home as in prism, when her liberties could be abridged by, the faculties remaining undeveloped by natural and con- genial use. It was time to understand that women were born for something more than marriage and maternity. There were human, social services which none could render more fitly than they, and there were duties now clumsily and inefficiently rendered by men that women, by reason of their very womanhood, were infinitely better qualified to discharge. Moreover,every day women were proving more and more completely their mental equality-with men. University lists revealed their equal, and often their superior power of acquire- ment and scholarship. They were advancing to equality in the school of medicine, and despite the bigotry and professional exchisivenees of the sterner sex,the day wasl at hand when they would gain places in the Royal Col- leges of Physic and Surgery. Nor could they much, longer be refused admission to University degrees even in the older halls of learning. But beyond that how clearly had women in recent years proved their capacity even for legislative functions upon School Boards and Boards of Guardians. In both these spheres they had obviously a distinct and desirable mission ; indeed be did not hesitate to say, from some considerable know- ledge of the necessities of the case, that as a guariian or the poor, a wise, matronly Christian woman might find a sphere of most useful service. How could a body of business men whose acquaintance with their own home life was confined chiefly to the hours that they spent at leisure or in bed be suonosed to know what was required for the internal domestic regulation of a large poor- house, where several hundreds of inmates (half of them women) required feeding. housing,clothing, nursing,and comforting in the destitution of age, sicknesss infirmity nd want?: Here was a peculiar sphere in which hrietian womanhood could render twice the service even of the most Christian manhood and the people of this country would do well by their poor ween they banded over the management of pauper homes to a majority of women rather than to an ignorant and in- capable monopoly of men. This was more for older women but young women whose physical strength and unselfishness of disposition continued to qualify them, were finding in the rapidly growing nursing sisterhoods a truly Christian sphere of helpful ministry. Other young woman were freely offering themselves as missionaries in foreign lands. The Salvation Army,said the preacher, had splendidly shown that immense heroic services could be rendered by women to lost and straying humanity in the slums of our large towns.lt used to be thought, and was thought yet by many, to be an essen- tially unwomanly thing for a woman to speak in pub- lic. A woman might act upon the stage attired in very sletder or very bedizened costume; she might sing in the opera. gain her livrng in the concert room or the music hall, and yet keep womanly. But if in the attire of ordinary life and in modest, earnest speech she ven- tured to stand before a mixed audience to testify for Christ, to protest against rampant impurity or degrad- ing intemperance, then the prudes, male and female, discerned the loss of womanly modesty and grace. So far, however,a e the new womanhood of the present day followed such lines as these it deserved not sneers but cheers. Speaking as he did to the young women of Wycliffe that night he confessed to a deep desire to see awakened among them an enthusiasm for Christ. And that meant an enthusiasm for an unselfish and service- able life—a willingness to forego self-indulgence, to con- quer nervous timidities, and to enlist in life's use- fulness. He said he thought that there was hardly a young woman there who did not despise those caricatures of womanhood which had largely be- come indentified with the title "The New Woman." Any womanhood which aimed to ape manhood, whether in similarity of costume, of tall, rollicking and unsavoury speech, of loud and reckless habit, of constantly living out of doors to the neglect of duty and the peril of re- putation, or that endorsed the ideas of freedom and licence suggested in some of the popular novels of the day—any such womanhood as that must meet with their complete disapprove] and reprobation. Their ambition should be to follekw in the steps of those women who did so lovingly ministr to Jesus and of their successors who in the early days of the Christian Church, " laboured ab- undantly in the Lord.,"

THE MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

While the Musical S3ciety is to be warmly con- gratulated in closiag its season's work with a con- cert which must be allotted a place of honour in the society's annals, it is much to be regretted that the public appreciation of the society's work as shewn by the attendance, is so scant and discourag- ing. From a musical point of view the society has never in its history shewn so solid a foundation of all-rouhd ability and efficiency as at the present - time,nor has the direction and training of its forces ever been in abler or more skilful hands. Why, therefore, there should not be a full and practical sympathy with the society's aims and objects shewn on the part of the musical public is a matter for serious consideration. That so much sweetness should be wasted on the desert air of a sparse audi- ence is deeply to be deplored and it is to be hoped that the lack of extrinsic support will in no way damp the ardour of the members of an institution now in the fulness of its functions and otherwise prosperous. The platform and choir gallery of the Parr Hall presented an imposing spectacle on Thursday week when Mr. Crossley took his seat at the conductor's desk. As at all the Society's recent concerts, there was a splendid gathering of choraP,members ; and as to the orchestra, we elo not remember to have seen so full a complement of in- struments at any previous concert in Warrington. For the complete performance of Mascagni's"Cavall- eria Rusticana " a fully equipped orchestra is, of course, necessary, and it was a novelty to find that every instrument down to the harp and the bells were ready for service. The principal vocalists were Miss Maggie Davies, (soprano), Miss Lilian Hovey, (contralto), Mr. liirwen Jones, (tenor), and Mr. David Hughes, (bass) ; and Mr. J. B. Caldwell, officiated as organist. Mendelssohn's " Walpurgis Night" having the first place in the programme, it is a pity that there was little opportunity, owing to the interruption of late-comers taking their seats, cf listening attentively to the overture which the composer has seen fit to develops at somewhat inordinate length. There is splen- did teope for the united voices in the choral numbers of this essentially dramatic cantata, Mendelssohn having left few works bear- ing stronger marks of originality than the descrip- tive ensemble" C3MO with torches" and the more subdued and pointed" Disperse, ye gallant men." In the first magnificent chorus, as indeed through- out the work, the Society's members showed a grasp of the music which resulted in a finished per- formance ot the entire cantata. The solo passages were in the hands of Miss Hovey, Mr. Jones and Mr. Hughes, whose declamation and vocalisation was most effective, special praise being due to Mr. Jones for his line rendering of the solo ending "Thy light shall shine for ever." In undertaking a concert-performance of Mas- cagni's opera " Cavalleria liasticana," the Musical Society might be pardoned some feeling of hesita- tion on account of the inherent difficulties which surround the task. Thanks, however, to the hard, though, let us hope not altogether unpleasurable work done at the rehearsals, the sequel more than justified the venture. Judged as a whole, Thurs- day's performance of this popular opera was some- thing the Society may reasonably feel proud of. There are doubtless passages for the orchestra, the execution of which it would, of course, be easy to take exception to, but these form only a small por- tion of a large whole which was so entirely satis- factory as to come as a pleasant surprise to those unacquainted with the capabilities of the Society's orchestra. And here it is only due to add a full meed of praise to Mr. Crossley. for the steadiness with which he so successfully piloted all concerned, through the many pitfalls inseparable trona a work of this character. The prelude and Turridu's song with harp accompaniment, at once inspired con- fidence ; and later, when the chorus first enters with the tuneful chorus descriptive of the joys of Spring it was seen that nothing had been left to chance in the training of the chorus. After Alfio's inspirit- ing song and its kindred chorus, the course of the opera brings the listener quickly to the chief choral number—the" Easter Hymn."—and the singing of the massed voices here reached a fine climax. A small;section of the choral members ably undertook the concerted music heard within the sanctuary to ' organ accompaniment, the bulk of the choral strength being reserved with impressive effect for the verse "Rejoice that the Lord has arisen." Then followed one of the most beautiful portions of the opera, in which Santuzza tells the story of her wrongs to Mother Lucia. The subsequent passages in which Turridu and Santuzza engage in mutual recriminations are interesting from the frequent recurrence of the motif which haunts 'with porten- tious persistence the former's footsteps, and is chiefly announced by the 'cellos in the orchestra. The charming Stornello subject of Lola's song strikes in with refreshing fervour, 'and here Mies Hovey sang in excellent taste. The exciting duet between the tenor and the soprano "Stay, Tar- ridu I" was presented with great dramatic force by Miss Davies and Mr. Jones, though later there was some faulty taking up of the vocal parts as the culmination of the story is reached immediately before the famous "Intermezzo." This movement was performed by the orchestra in splendidistyle andlin response to a loudly expressed encore it was repeated. The charming Now homeward returning" and the energetic " Viva " choruses brilliantly concluded the work of the chorus, and-the more tragic portions of the opera falling chiefly to the lot of the tenors and bass, quickly brought the work to a highly dramatic close. This initial effort in essaying the concert-perform- ance of opera must be pronounced so unqualified a success from the Society's point of view that we earnestly hope it may be encouraged to further achievements in this direction. Mr. Alfred R. Sutton's chorus with orchestral accompaniment " The Woodland Brook" received a fine interpreta- tion at the hands of the Socie ;y. The verses have been treated by Mr. Sutton in a free and graceful manner and the mu-ic is appropriately bright and 9parkling. The cordial reception accorded this item must have been highly gratifying to the composer. ' Miss Maggie Davies sang in the first part of the programme" Ah fors' 6 lui " from "La Traviata " with a dash and verve which called forth quite a storm of applause to which she was constrained to repeatedly bow her acknowledgments.

TOWNSHIP MEETINGS.

WARRINGTON.

_ The annual township ineeting for Warrington_ was held in the Sessions house, Irlam-st., on Thursday last week. There were present Councillor' Shaw-Green (in the chair), Woods, and Bennett, and Messrs. John Fairhurst, Thomas Moores, J. L. Tunstall (assistant overseer), and Joseph Smith (clerk). • Accounts for the two half- years ending 25th March and 29th September, 1895,were read by Mr. Tunstall. They showed that the balance in hand at the beginning of the first half-year was £2,669 ss. 2d. ; amount received from rates £19,993 12s. 9J.; from other sources, £593 143. 91., making a total of £23,255 12s. Bd. The contributions for rates and ex- penses amounted to £21028 153. 4,1., leaving a balance at the end of September; 1895, of 622,227 17s. 4d. The accounts Of the sanitary special expenses showed that the year commenced with ,a balance in hand of £2O 123. 33. ; receipts from rates amounted to £lO5 4s. id., and after meeting contributions and expenses amount- ing to £9l. 3.1.8. Bd., there. was a balance in hand of £34 4s. Bd. The county rates account showed a balance to commence with of £97 11s. £ll4 Os. 4d. was received from rate's and a total amount of £2ll 114. 4d. was paid in contributions and other expenses. This acceant is now closed. The Chairman proposed the adoption of the accounts.—Mr. Morres se3onded the proposition,whicti was carried. A return of the full and fair annual valuation of property voitliin the township of Warrington for tht.. purpose of submitting it to the County Rate Committee was.also read and approved of. The total valuation of the township ameunted in -the gross to £201,974 64. ; rateable £163,139 10s. The Chairman in answer to en quiries with regard to the estimates for de poor rate said he had very great pleasure in stating that although the Corporation had called for a larger contribution the overseers proposed to levy a rate for the coming year of the same-amount as that of last year, which would be 2s. 81. in the £. •

letter to Mr Obitor.

THE LEPERS IN CYPRUS.

Sir,—l hope that you will give me your aid in making an appeal on behalf of a much-afflicted but little-known community. The following letter is being circulated in Cyprus : "The lepers in the Cyprus Leper Farm, members of the Orthodox Greek Church, numbering about eighty, having collected from their small daily pittance a few pounds with the view of building a church, which is greatly needed by them—and which scheme is one of their greatest ambitions—l appeal to the benevolent on their behalf, so as to enable them to carry out their wishes. Any subscription sent to me will be thankfully acknowledged. "F. C. HEIDENSTAM. "Address, F. C. Heidenstans, C. M.G., Chief Medical Officer With the Government of Cyprus. and Medical Officer in charge of the Leper Farm, Nicosia, Cyprus." A beautiful and generally healthy climate does not pre- vent the existence of this disease so prevalent in the East. In the island all certified lepers are obliged to live in the Leper Farm, near to Nicosia. Their numbers, happily, are not large. I visited the farm twice very re- cently,in February and March. There were then one hun- dred and two lepers living there; of these, eighty are of the Greek Orthodox Church,two are Maronites. eighteen are Mohammedans, and two are what are called Lino- bambakos,i.e., half Greek Christians ,half Mohammedans or Greek Christians or Mohammedans. according to their surroundings. These Linobambakos attend the Greek Church Service in the Leper Farm. At present the services are celebrated in a room of one of the old mud huts, most of the little houses where the lepers are lodged being of good stone. When one con- siders the awful disease from which these poor people suffer, one easily understands that they must turn to their religion for consolation and hope. The eighty who are of the Greek Orthodox faith long to have a real church to go to, such as they would have if they were not prisoners of necessity. Their priest, an exceptionally intelligent-looking and young man, lives with them. He is not a leper. The sum of money wanted for building their little church is not large, only some three hundred pounds. There is in hand about thirty pounds. In order that those who have not seen lepers may know something of their afflictions,let me mention some of the cases in the Leper Farm. There are several who, besides rotting away ftom leprosy,are hopelessly paraly- tic and have to be lifted and carried by other lepers who are in better case. Many have lost the sight of one eye, many of both eyes. Many are gradually losing their fingers or their toes,their hands or their feet. Of these I noted one man who has one hand devoid of fingers ; the hand itself will aeon fall off at the wrist. Many are consumptive ; one has cancer. There is a young woman who,nut being a leper,insisted on accompanying her hus- band to the farm when he had been declared a leper. She is now a leper, and has lost the sight of one eye. There is one man who has white leprosy; he is a paralytic. Is it strange that people so grievously afflicted, who are by nature and training religious, are filled with a deshe to have a church instead of a room to worship in, which those who have their sight can see and describe to those who are blind? The people in Cyprus are gener- ally very poor, and are heavily taxed. This is my apo- logy for making this appeal. I hope that there are many kind people in this country who, in compassion for the sorrows of the little leper community, will subscribe to- wards their church. Lady Sendall (Government House. Nicosia) and Dr. Beidenstam, (chief Medical Officer. Nicosia) will gladly receive subscriptions. I also shall be most happy 'to receive and remit them ; or they can be paid to the account of the "Leper Church Fund," at Parr's Banking Company and the Alliance Bank. I am, Sir, your obedient servant. ROBERT PIERPOINT.

House of Commoms, April 8. P.S.—I have received the following letter : " Hawarden, April 7, 1696. " Dear Sir,—Though I am in no way qualified to ad- vise upon particulars, I think that the plan you have projected is of extreme interest,and I am only prevented from offPring a contribution to it by the fact that I have undertaken single-handed a plan on somewhat beyond the usual scale which compels me to withhold support except from cases which may be called proximate. "Your very faithful and obedient, W. E. GLADSTONE.

" R. Pierpoint, Esq."

FRODSHAM.

SUICIDE AT KINGSLEY.—On Tuesday morning, Joseph Dutton, aged 30, of Kingsley near Frodsham, was found on Wednesday lying from the effects of poisoning by carbolic acid. Deceased has suffered very much from influenzl and on Monday went to visit a brother-in-law at Liverpool. He returned home the same evening, ap- parently all right. Next morning his wife left him in bed, and on returning shortly afterwards found him in great pain, having drunk a large quantity of carbolic acid kept for disinfectant purposes. Medical aid was summoned but the man died the same morning On Wed- nesday afternoon Mr. H. Churton held an inquest at the Horse Shoe Inn ,Kingsley, on the body of George Dutton, aged 30 years,labourer,who died from the effects of tak- ing carbolic acid the previous morning.—A verdict of suicide whilst temporarily insane was returned. The Coroner said that carbolic acid was one of the unpro- tected poisons,and chemists were allowed to sell it to anyone without requiring signature of witnesses. He had written to the Secretary of State on the subject •over and over again and nothing had been done. HALTON PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.

At a meeting of the above committee held on Satur- day under the presieency of the Rev. G. D. Wray, there were present Messrs. Wright, Looker, Ackerley, Jeacock and Barrow. The Clerk read a letter from the Local Government Board and a report from Dr. Vacher, with reference to Halton Brook, and it was resolved on the motion of Mr. Looker, seconded by Mr. Barrow,that the suggestion in the report to have the settling tanks fre- quently cleansed and lined be carried out and that this be reported to the Local Government Board. It was de- cided on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Jeacock, that the attention of the Inspector of Nuisance be called to a case of overcrowding of a house, and that he be instructed to take the necessary steps for abate- ment of the nuisance. The minutes were adopted. MEDICAL OFFICER'S REEORT.

Dr. Adams, the medical officer, reported that a case of scarlet fever had occurred at Stockton Heath. The pati, ent agedl9 years, worked at Warrington and is believed to have contracted the disease there. He had visited the premises affected and issued the usual preventive orders and the premises, bedding, Ric., would be disinfected in due course.

THE CLERK'S SALARY.

The Local Government Board wrote sanctioning the payment to the clerk of £250 for his services to the Rural District Council during the year ending March 23rd.

THE LATCHFORD LOCKS.

Mr. Woodcock, clerk to the Grappenhall Parish Conn- cil,wrote stating that at a meeting of the Council held on the 30th inst. he was instructed -to write to press the matter re Latchford locks and ask them to press the matter upon the Canal Company at once as another ac- cident (which might have had a fatal termination had not timely help been at hand) ha& happened since the last communication.—The Surveyor, Mr. Swinton, re- ported t"at the committee appointed to inspect the foot- pa'h met on Monday, the 13:h inst., at 3 o'clock in the afternoon, present Admiral Jones Parry, Messrs. W. Long, J. Mclntyre, W. Bradford, G. Pilling, R. Warbni- ton, and the surveyor. The committee inspected the various uncertain roads across the locks and were unani- mous in recommending the Council to request the canal firstly to provide a more defined road which the com- mittee respectfully suggest should be across the centre gates of the locks. Secondly to fence the road on both sides, for the whole length of the Company's property between the road numbered on the deposited plans 105 and 106, and to sufficiently light at all times certain lengths which the committee think may be impracticable for the company to have a fence that is at the Lock walls. Thirdly to erect clapgates at each end of the footway suitable for pedestrians. Fourthly to maintain a better and more even surface on the footway down the embankments of the canal to the Lock sides.

The Chairman moved the adoption of the report, and said that there were great difficulties in the way of fenc- ing the foothpaths. Mr. Pilling seconded and the resolu- tions were Carried.

This was all the business of public importance.

CHAIWE AGAINST A RUNCORN • LANDLORD. CELEBRATING A BIRTHDAY.

At Runcorn Petty Sessions on Mondav,William Young was charged with selling intoxicants during prohibited hours in the Cornwall and Devonshire Arms on the morning of the 24th March. Frederick Lang!ey,Percival Lane, John Edward Johnson, 105 Shaw-3t., John Tom- linson, Surrey-st., Thomas J. Washburn, Surrey-st., and Sarah Price were charged with unlawfully being on the premises of the first defendant.—Mr. Giles, of Chester, prosecuted on behalf of the police, and Mr. A. Browne (Warrington), defended.—Seageaut Lee said that on the morning of the 24th March he went in company with P.C.'s Booth and Butler to the Cornwall and Devonshire Arms. The time would be about 1.15. He went to the tap room window in Regent-st., and he heard the voices of two females and one male. He heard one of the females leave the room and afterwards heard her go across the bar towards the kitchen. He then heard a shuffling going on in the taproom, and a noise as if the furniture was moving about. On going round the back with Constable Booth, he got into the back premises,and stood against a partition in the yard. There was a light in the kitchen the door of which was open. They could hear men and women's voices speaking loudly. He left Booth and went back to Butler, and they knocked at the door, which was opened by Mrs. Young the landlord's mother.On going into the house, he spoke to Mrs. Young and she replied. He went to the taproom but there was no one in. He then went to the kitchen where he found three men, John Tomlinson, Thomas J. Woodburn, Fred Langley and Mrs. Young. On the table there were five empty glasses,a half gallon jug and two plates with beer on them. The floor was wet, and there was a strong smell of ale. In answer to the question as to what they had done with the beer. Washburn said" It had been thrown on the floor." He then asked Mrs. Young, what she was doing with the men on the premises. She hesi- tated a bit and then said, "It is my birthnay and we are keeping it up." From what he had heard before he asked if there', was anyone else in the house and she said "No." P.C. Butler came in just then and said he could hear voices upstairs and Mrs. Young said that was her daughter and the lodger. He went upstairs and found the lodger in bed asleep br himself. He heard a female voice in the other room and on opening the door found Sarah Price fully dressed. She was under the influence of drink. In answer to a question as what she was doing there,she said laughingly, " I wag" Frying to run out of your way." He then went downstairs and asked Mrs. Young where her daughter was and she said she did not know. He went into the parlour and found Carrie Young, 16 years of age, and John Edward Johnson. Mrs. Young said that the girl was her daughter and the man a friend of theirs. Mrs. Young afterwards said her husband was at Southport looking for work. The defendants were not drunk. Cross-examined he did not know that one of the defendants was teetotal, nor that two of them ere engaged to Mrs. Young's daughters. tie was not aware that Mrs. Price had been charing at the house all day. P.C.'s Butler and Booth also gave evidence. Mr. Browne, for the defence, said that they did not dispute the evidence of the police. If Mrs. Young had only sent word to the police that she was going to keep up the birthday party this case would never have had to be heard. Even in Runcorn, he believed landlords were allowed to have birthdays and to keep them up. He submitted that all the young men charged had been asked by Mrs. Young to supper as her birthday oc- curred on March 23rd. The reason that they were there was that two of the defendants were engaged to Mrs. Young's daughters. Mrs. Price had been cleaning there all day, and Mrs. Young asked her to stay to supper.— Evidence for the defence was then given by Mrs. Price, Ellen Young, mother of defendant, and Ellen Young, sister of defendant. Carrie Yaung, and the defendants Washburne, Tomlinson, Johnson and Langley.—The magistrates, in giving their decision said that they con- sidered that the police were perfectly justified with the course they had taken, but they did not consider there was sufficient evidence to convict.—The case was ac- cordingly dismissed.

WARRINGTON RUGBY FOOTBALL WORKS COMPETITION.

This competition hr medals!itmongst the works of the town has been continued each week-night during the present week. As the competition advances greater in- terest is taken in the matches, and there have been large numbers of spectators to witness them. WAGON WORKS (C.L.C.) V. WHITECROSS.—Teams from these works met on Monday evening. The result of a good gome was a victory for the Wagon Works men by one goal, two tries, to ene try. They started general favourites, and are considered very probable winners of the medals. Their next engagement will be with the Pearson and Knowles team. Siddall, of the Whitecross team, sustained serious injuries to his skull. WARRINGSON L. AND N.-W. RAILWAY V. CORPORATION. —This match on Tuesday evening was somewhat marred by a heavy downpour of rain. The Corporation team wan expected to make a good display, but the very opposite was the case, and it was apparent that several members of the team were unaware of the rules of the game. Broady and Bate were absent from the bea en team. The result was that the railway men had quite an easy task, and ran out winners by three goals, two tries to nil. In the next round P.Walker and Son's team will be their opponents._ . .

PEARSON AND KNO,iVLES V. CROSFIELD'S.—These teams met in the second round of the competition on Wednes- day night. The Pearson and Knowles'fifteen included such well-kn,wn players as Cross, Hill, Scholtz, Thornley and Houghton. For the first twenty minutes of the game, play was very even and then a regrettable accident oc- curred. J. Massey, a wing three-quarter for Crosfield's team collided with considerable force with Rowe, the opponents full hack, and broke his collar-bone in two places. Sergeant RowbAtom, rendered first aid to the injured player. His retirement handicapped :the Soap Works' team, and the Pearson and Knowles team soon afterwards commenced scoring. Cunningham after a speedy run, scored a try at the ccrner. Houghton soon afterwards followed with another try. On changing ends, the forgemon were leading by two tries to nil. During the second half they had most of the play, and Foster, Hughes, and Gibson each scored tries. Cross was successful in two of the place kicks, so that the scors at the close ran Pearson and Knowles two goals, three tries. Crosfields nil. Tho latter team was exceA- ingly unfortunate in losing Massey so early in the match. In Longmore they had a splendid full-back. With re- gard to the winning team it is just questionable whether they did not exceed the limit of first team men al- lowed.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT. (Continued from Page 3.)

THURSDIY.-THE BUDGET.

In the House of Commons, in reply to Mr. Hogan. Sir M. WHITE RIDLEY said the number of fatal accidents to cyclists in the Metropolitan Police district during the first quarter of the year was three, while there had been twelve serious accidents. In two cases only were the injured persons women. Asked by Mr. KNOWLE3 if he would introduce at an early date a Bill to carry out the report from the De- partmental Committee on the treatment of inebriates. Sir M. WatrE RIDLEY said the Bill was in draft but he could not say when he would be able to introduce it. Mr. W. H. LONG, answering Mr. Knox, said that with a view to guard against the introduction of swine fever into Lancashire the local authority had made regulations prohibiting the movement of swine into the administra- tive county, except for slaughter. He did not feel justi- fied in asking the local authority to remove the festric- tion.

Mr. BALFOUR informed Mr. Holland that the constitu- tion of the Committee on old-age pensions was not yet complete. The Chancellor of the Exchequer, in making his annual financial statement, said the year just concluded had been financially speaking, a most remarkable year. The amount that had flowed into the Exchequer had been greater, he believed, than in any previous twelve months, and in spite of an expenditure which had been larger than any since the great war, the surplus was one of the largest ever known. The Exchequer receipts had been £5,812,000 in excess of the Estimates made by Sir W. Harcourt a year ago, and £7,290,000 more than the Exchequer receipts of the previous year. The Post-office and telegraph services showed a largo and sustained in- crease; house duty showed an increase of £500.000 ; and the net receipts from foreign spirits were £137,000 more than the estimate. Tea showed an increase of £158,000 over the receipts for the previous year, and the receipts from tobacco had been £333,000 m)re than the net receipts in the previous year. He believed the inareass from tobacco was mainly due to the great increase in the consumption of cigarettes. The receipts for wine, beer and spirits in 1894-5 had been exceeded by £136,000, £617,000, and £334,000 respectively. The exchequer receipts from the Death Duties had amounted to £11,600,000, besides £2,450,000 paid to the Local Taxation Account. They showed an increase of £2,460,000 over the estimate, but he believed that in regard to those duties the highest point had been reached. Under the heading of general stamps the estimate had been exceeded by £1,690,000, a result largely due to the wonderful fit of gambling on the Stock Exchange which would be fresh in the minds of all mem- bers. The net surplus for the year was £4,2lo,ooo,which had been devoted to naval works. After referring to the steps taken to improve the condition of the gold coinage, Sir M. HICKS-BEACH said that during the past year the National debt had been reduced by £8,131,000. The increase in the deposits in the savings banks showed the prosperous state of the country, but he was afraid that the State could not go on paying the pre- sent rate of interest. The revenue went on increasing, but the demands on it increased faster than the re- venue. He estimated that for the coming year the expenditure would amount to £100,047,000, an increase of £4,066,000 over the estimate of last year That increase was due mainly to the vast augmen- tation of the Navy Estimates. The revenue he expected to reach ,£101,755,000, which would give him a surplus of £1,708,000. lie proposed to spend about £200,000 in redressing certain hardships which had been found to occur in the working of the Finance Act of last year with regard to the legacy and succession duty ; and the bulk of the remaining £1,500,000 it was proposed to de- vote to the relief of agriculture. The reduction of the land tax from 43. 7.0 Is. would absorb £lOO,OOO, and £975,000 was to go to lighten the rates on agricultural land. The remaining £433,000 he proposed to use to meet the increased expenditure with regard to education which would fall within the present year on account of the proposals in the Education Bill.

STOCKTON HEATH.

PRESENTATION TO THE VICAR.—On Monday evening, several of the committee of the Young Men's Class, with their teacher, Mr. Hallows, on behalt of the members presented the Vicar with a very handsome gold pen and pencil case, a large size prayer book, and a musical album, all suitably inscribed. The presentation was made at the Vicarage, and Mr. Hallows said they had met on behalf of the class to express their good wishes to the Vicar on his birthday, hoping there were many more in store for him, and that each succeeding one might be happier than the last, and that he might soon be res- tored to such health and strength as to enable him to lead them on in the work, as in the happy times of yore. Mr. J. Spensley made the presentation, and referred to the heartiness with which the matter had been taken up by the members. and to the kindly feeling entertained for the Vicar by all the parishioners.—The Vicar, who was very deeply moved, thanked them very much for their handsome presents, and for the kindly feelings shown towards himself. He wished he had been able to get amongst them, and to help them more in their good work, but they knew:the reason. Now, acting on the doctor's orders, and in compliance with the wishes of his best friends, he had decided, with the consent of the Bishop, to take an entire rest, which he hoped, with the blessing of God, would result in him being able to take up the work of the parish with greater zeal than he had ever done before.—Mr. Baldwin and Mr. Miller both spoke of the kindness and courtesy of the Vicar, and ex- pressed hopes of his early recovery.—Afterwards about an hour was spent in pleasant chat and a little music.

HALTON.

CYCLISTS AND THH "BROW."—The N.C.U.bave deemed it necessary to erect a danger board on the brow of Hal- ton Hill. This is not meant as a warning to local cyc ists so much as a warning to strangers to the district, who are not used to the road. To the local cyclists the incline is not considered exactly dangerous, but there is a "ticklish "corner half way down which is somewhat trying when travelling down the incline at a high rate of speed.

WARRINGTON.

PRIMROSE LEAGUE MEETING AT LATCHFORD.

On Saturday evening the annual meeting of the Grappenhall Habitation of the Primrose League was held in the Parish Room,Latchford. There was only a small attendance. The chair was taken by Mr. J. C. Parr, J.P., D.L. Mr. A. L. Kaye read the secretary's report and the chairman remarked that it was fairly satisfactory. On his proposition it was adopted and a vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Kaye, the secretary. Mr. Kaye also read the financial report in the absence of Mrs. Coxon, the treasurer, and said that the total expenditure was .254 13s. id., and the income £49 13s. id., leaving a debt to the treasurer of £5. This report was adopted, and Mrs. Coxon thanked for her services. The following officers were then elected :—Mrs. Parr, ruling councillor; Mrs. Coxon, treasurer ; Mr. Roger Parr, secretary ; wardens for Latchford, Mr. E. Hankey, Mr, T. liankey, Mr. Howard, Mr. Lay- land; Grappenhall, Messrs. Spence, Fairhurst,Long- shaw, and A. L. Kaye; Lumb Brook, Mr. H. Rough- sedge. rhe Appleton and Stretton habitations were joined together by the request of the former. Votes of thanks to the wardens and the chairman concluded the business. The remaining part of the evening was occupied by Mr. Mel 13. Spurr, the highly popular entertainer, and he gave unqua- lified pleasure to all with his songs, sketches, and recitals. EVERY CYCLIST should use JOHN S. GREEN'S NEW ROAD MAP,6O miles about WarringLon,lB.6d. nett, at 34, Bridge-st. and 23, Buttermarket-st.—Advt. Y.M.C.A. P.S.A.—OPEN AIRETING.

Last Sunday being prize day, the P.S.A. meeting was an open one, and there was a large attendance of members and friends. Mr. William Owen, J.P., presided, and congratulated the committee on the success of the movement, and on the regular and increasing attendances of the members. The prizes which had been won by those members making 12 attendances:in the quarter, represented more than 90 per cent.of the total membership, which in itself was a proof that the members took deep interest in the work, and that there was a large amount of good done. The meetings he was pleased to see, were kept up all the year round, and were as well attended in summer as winter. Mr. Arthur Kent, of Manchester, gave an address, taking as his sub- ject " Apartments to let." He pointed out that the minds of people could be furnished with good and useful things, or left empty and desolate, according to the way they were looked after and attended to. Miss Kent saLg with much taste "The Last Mile- stone," and Mr. Hitches gave a clarionet solo in good style, being accompanied by Mr. F. Hickman. Mr. Kent (president) and Mr. Barber also took part in toe meeting, which was one of the most successful of the" prize days "which has been held. HOUSE COMMITTEE.

The minutes of this committee mentioned the passing of a vote of thanks to the chairman and vice-chairman of the c&mmittee (Mr. Clarke and Mr. A. J. Jolley re- spectively) for the manner they had performed their duties during the past year. A similar comrliment was paid to the master and matron, and officers of the Work- house.—The minutes were confirmed on the motion of Mr. Clarke, seconded by Mr. A. J. Jolley.

SCHOOLS COMMITTEE.

This committee reported that Mr. Forshaw had ex- amined the boiler at Padgate schools, and advised that the leakage should be attended to at once. A vote of thanks was passed to the chairman and vice-chairman, on the proposition of Mrs. Clay,for the valuable services rendered by them and the general regularity of their at- tendancs. The minutes were passed ota. he motion of Mr. A. J. Jolley, seconded by Mrs. Clay.—The Clerk re- ported that he had received a report from the Boiler In- surance Company on the state of the boiler, and it was desirable that it should have immediate attention.—lt was decided on the proposition of Mr. Thompson that Mr. Forshaw should submit an estimate of the cost of the repair of the boiler.

GIFT TO TIIE INMATES.

The SUPERINTENDENT reported that he had received a box of oranges as an Easter gift from Mr. William Floughton.

THANKS TO THE CHAIRMAN AND VICE-CHAIRMEN.

Mr. A. J. JoLLEY propoed a hearty vote of thanks to the chairman and the vice-chairmen. He (Mr. Jolley) th3u.ht their Chairman had filled the post with credit to the Board and himself. If they looked over the history of that Board they would find that the chair had been filled by men of real eminence and force of character, and he belt eyed Mr. Houghton had not fallen below the high standard of his predecessors. With regard to the vice-chairmen, he might say that the Chairman had been there so constantly that they had had very little to do. But they deserved the thanks of the Board.—Mr. Clarke seconded th^ resolution, and Mr. Hickman and Father Moore supported it. The resolution having been passed, the Chairman in the course of his acknowledgments,said he could not leave the chair without paying a compli- ment to the Clerk. Guardians might come and go but the Clerk went on for ever. (Laughter.) He hoped their Clerk would continue his duties for many years to ceme. (Hear, hear.)

THE LADIES COMMITTEE.

The CHAIRMAN proposed a vote of thanks to the Ladies Committee for their work during the year. He was sure they had done their work with the very best in- tention.—Mr. THOMPSON seconded the proposition. He had confidence at the time when he moved the aproint- ment of the Ladies Committee, that the Board would be quite pleased with what they din. He thought the Board did a wise thing in ap- pointing the committee.—Mr. CLARKE supported the vote, and said he was sure the ladies had been very use- ful, and hat their work would result in much good.— The Clerk on being asked by the Chairman to speak, said in a few weeks he should enter upon his 25th year of ser- vice as Clerk. Previous to being Clerk he was a member of the Board for some years. He had had over fifty years connection with the Board. He concurred with the re- marks made by Mr. Thompson with reference to the Ladies Committee,and he thought that committee would eventually be for the, good of the institutions of that Union. During his term of service on the Board he had tried to see that the wishes of the Board were carried out in an honourable and impartial manner. He thanked the Board for their constant kindness and courtesy to himself.—The vote was unanimously agreed to, and the Board rose.

BILLIARDS.

LIBERAL v. CONSERVATIVE CLUBS.

On Tuesday the return match between teams repre- senting the two political clubs of the town was played off at the Conservative Club, Sankey-st. On the previous occasion when the first round was played at the Liberal Club the homesters won by a majority of 165. A large amount of interest was,therefore, taken in the result,the Conservative Club being crowded. Tables in both rooms were used and some very good play witnessed, success falling to the endeavours of the homesters. As before the greatest interest centred in the game between Mr. A. H. Crosfield and Mr. E. Parry. The latter gentleman when playing at the Liberal Club only scored 55, while his opponent put on 150, but his chances were thought to be favourable for a much better nerformance when "at home." On starting he went off at a good speed. and held the lead up to about 50, when Mr. Crosfield accustomed himself to the run of the table and compiled a break of 31. This was followed by smaller ones in quick succession, and he ultimately reached his points with Mr. Parry's score at 81. The result was very heartily cheered by players on both sides and congratulations for the winner were general. The following are the scores of both the borne and away matches with the results and the majority on the two games for the Conservatives :

Mr. GEORGE PILLING, hon. secretary of the Conserva- tive club, observed that all the arrangements had been conducted in a friendlyand gentlemanly manner. The Conservatives would be pleased to make the event an annual one. (Hear, hear.) So long as he was in office at any rate, he would do all that lay in his power to make the proceedings as pleasant as possible. cAp- plause.) Alderman SUTTON, J.P., gave a hearty welcome to the Liberals, and expressed the pleasure it afforded him to see both parties meet in such a friendly manner. His opinion was that even in political matters they ought to be friendly towaris each other, and meet as if no differ- ence of opinion really existed. He proposed that a vote of thanks b. given to the Liberals for coming amongst them that evening. He coupled with the vote the names of Mr. Thomas Morris and Councillor Tinnion. (Applause.) The vote was heartily agreed to. Mr. Moms, who was warmly received, said he was pleased to be able to refer to the sterling and honest conduct of the Conservatives generally. Their politics were awfully hard during a contest, but when it was over they put them away and were very cordial. The billiard match was a wise arrangement on the part of the leaders of tbe two clubs, and it was an excellent thing to bring the members in social competition before the arrival of the bowling season. He hoped the matches would be continued as long as the political clubs existed in Warrington. (Applause.) Councillor TINNION also responded, and congratulated the Conservatives on their victory after some good play. The Liberals were thoroughly satisfied with the beating they had received, though he hoped they would be able when tha. next game was played to say that they had won. (Laughter and applause.) He could say as one who had taken part in a considerable amount of politi- cal fighting in Warrington that it had given him much pleasure to put away the weapons of warfare and meet in such a friendly spirit that evening. He thoroughly believed that these billiard competitions in the winter and the bowling ones in the summer would do a great deal to heal some of the little sores that arose in elec- tioneering times. (Applause.) Three hearty cheers having been given for the victors, the proceedings were brought to a conclusion.

A letter from Allahabad throws some light upon the cause of the heavy mortality in the Indian army. The Norfolk Regiment has lost 20 men by enteric fever since March 1, and the Royal Irish lost their colonel and seve- ral men from the same cause. Enteric fever now ac counts annually for one-third of the total deaths occur. ring in the British army in India. LAWN MOWERS.—CfAerawn, Bridge-street, War rington,—ildvt.

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The improved position in the Northern Union compe- tition which Warrington gained last week, was fully maintained and buttressed up on Saturday, when War- rington for the second time this season defeated the Wakefield Trinity fifteen. The first engagement was played at the latter end of October, awl Warrington then won by seven points to nil, and on Saturday the margin was again the same, Warrington being ten points and Wakefield three. It was, of course, rather unfortunate for the Trinitarians that they should be obliged to travel to Warrington on both occasions ow- ing to the suspension of their ground on account of mis- bellaviour on the part of their specLators.

Of Saturday's game it cannot be said that it was one of the most pleasant to witness. The play was confined too much to the forward ranks, and if we mistake not, the half backs played rather selfishly. Bate was missed in this department, and it is to be hoped that he will have sufficiently recovered from his injuries, to don the jersey again before the close season commences.

Of the three-quarter line, it must be admitted that it was not one from which great thirgs could be expected. With absentees of such merit as Barber, Cross,and Law- less,,he threequarters were not very formidable. They, however.were quite as smart as their opponents, Burton especially being a shining light. The visiting team possessed the best fomards,as Yorkshire clubs generally do,and it is in this department that Halifax may be ex- pected to carry off the palm next Saturday.

With regard to the Halifax team,it may be stated that they have now only two matches to play. On the 20% th.,y are to meet Runcorn on the Irwell-lane enclosure, and on the following Saturday they will have Warring- ton as their opponents. Both teams have already been beaten this season by Halifax. It is stated in the " Athletic News " that it will be a large order for Hali- Dx to beat Runcorn, but no mention is made of the Warrington engagement. There is just a probability that the Halifax men may find it quite as large an order to defeat Warrington as their Cheshire neighbours— Runcorn.

There will doubtless be a large " gate " at Widnes to- day, when Warrington play their return engagement with the reprecentatives ot the chemical town. Owing to the recent defeats of the Widnes team Warrington occupy a far better position in the League tables,and the " wirepullers " intend to make every effort to maintain their lead. Warrington will be represented by the fol- lowing :—Heesom, full back ; Barber, Burton, Cross,and Broady, threequarter backs ; Higginbottom and Hough- ton, half backs ; Nevins, Taylor, Dakin, Sanders, Bur- rows, Berry, Fairhurst, and Thornton, forwards.

Brlughton Rasers secured their first two points in the Lancashire section of the competition on Monday, when they decisively beat Widnes. Warrington are due on the Rangers' ground on Monday night, and it is to be hoped that they will come out of the engagement with greater credit than the Widnesians It cannot be said that Warrington have done very well in their away engagements, having only won three during the sea- son, but with a good team on Monday another should be added to the list of victories.

A meeting of the Lancashire Section was held in Man- chester on Tuesday evening. The chief item was the charge against Priestley and Barker, two Liversedge players, of having used abusive language to Mr. Dillon, of Warrington, who was referee in the match against Leigh on March 29. The two players and Mr. Dillon were present and gave evidence. Other Liversedge players were incriminated, but could not be traced.— After consideration of the facts, the committee decided to suspend Priestley and Barker over this season and two months in next—that is, to the end of October. The facts of the chaige generally were admitted by the Liversedge representative, Mr. Hampshire, who was instructed to caution all their players as to future con- duet. Alford, Widnes A team, for striking an opponent was dismissed with a reprimand. Archer was trans- ferred from Widnes to St. Helens, and Catterall given permission to play with Wigan. It was ascertained that Walkden and St. Helens Recreation have made formal application to join the Union.

ASSOCIATION FOOTBALL.

The re-played match for the if;O-c-i-ailon Cup,on Satur- day, between St. Elphins and Barnton Rovers has re- sulted for the second time in the Barntonians carrying off the trophy. St. Elphin's made strenuous endeavours to score but were unable to raise a point while their op- ponents scored 3 goals. At half time nothing had been scored,and it was thought that another draw was going to be made, but on the change of sides Baraton with some little wind advantage soon asserted their superi- ority. In passing they were the smarter team and their victory was earned.

From a spectator's point of view, the match was spoiled by the hQavy cross-wind carrying the ball into touch and baffling the players. With the Barnton team there came a large contingent of vilagers, and these, many of them women, kept up a continual stream of confident assertions as to the visitors superiority. Un- fortunately for home prestige they were right, and the cup only wants to be won again by Barnton when it be- comes their absolute property.

CRICKET.

Very shortly we shall bid adieu to football for a sea- son, and attention will then be directed to cricket. The Warrington Cricket Club fixtures were published in this journal last week, and it would be seen that some in- teresting engagements are to take place. For the first part of the season the team will be under the captaincy of Mr. S. M. Crosfield, who, it is stated, intends to pay a visit to Iceland about midsummer. The professionals for this season are A. Thewlis and George Scrimshaw. The former comes from Yorkshire and has an excellent batting average, whilst the last-mentioned is said to be very good with the ball. The absence of Dr. Reginald Smith from the team is to be regretted, but still the e is every prospect of a successful season for the Warring- ton eleven, which is acknowledged to be one of the best in the district.

===== 0002601_18960418_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
SWIMMING.

The Warrington Polo team will figure in the" A " division of the competitions in connection with the Liverpool and District Polo Association during the forth- coming season, and some interesting engagements should be witnessed. The Warrington team will again be under the captaincy of Mr. T. Locker, and Mr. Scot- son will be the sub-captain. Their opponents in the competition will include Sefton. North Liverpool, West Liverpool, and St. Helens. It will be remembered that the latter club beat the Warrington polo team in both engagements last season, but Warrington are hoping to be able to give a better account of themselves on the nest occasion.

CYCLING.

On Saturday about a dozen members of the Warring- ton Cycle Club turned out for a run to Cranage, for the first time this season. The journey going was very pleasant, and on arrival at the farm tea was partaken of. Meanwhile, however, the heavens had oer'cast and before 7 o'cicck there were signs of rain. This caused the wheelers to turn their noses homewards,but alas! the the decision had come too late to save them from an un- exampled drenching, and, of course, they arrived home just as the downpour ceased. It is always so. Twenty miles in the rain is very unpleasant under any circum- tances, but when the rain ceases at the journey's end, and you reflect that had you waited you would have been dry, it is all the more tantalising.

MAKE LIFE ENJOYABLE. SI Loss of health means loss of all which makes life en- joyable ; for, in addition to the bodily Fain and suffering which it causer, it entails a vast amount of mental pain and anxiety, especially in the homes of the working classes. It brings in its train a host of discomforts which retard the recovery of the sufferer, and hinder, instead of assisting, Nature to perform her functions. When a working man suffers from any ailment which prevents his following his daily avocation, he is natur- ally filled with anxiety regarding the maintenance of those depending on him for their daily sustenance. His very love for his family retards and hinders his speedy recovery, and his spirits become depres ed at the antici- pation of the gloomy prospects before them in the event of a long continuance of his illness. It is well known that depression of spirits ofpn proves a serious bar to a speedy recovery. These results arise in addition to the various incon- veniences and discomforts which the sufferer and his family experience as direct results of his illness, such as additional expenses for luxuries for the sick, with in- creased difficulty to obtain those luxuries, or even the necessities of life. I Most of the various forms of disease which trouble the labouring classes of the country, and which are often attributable, more or less, to some unhealthly condition of their daily work, may be classed under a few leading heads. To avoid the discomforts arising from various forms of disease,every working man should seek the aid of THE VEGETABLE TONIC, Which has gained a world-wide renown as the most wonderful medicinal discovery of the age; and as un- failing remedy it is prescribed by many leading physi- cians in various ailments.
